FOREWORD
Of the great ancient works of Tamilnad known for their moral profundity
and weighty comments on life and thought, Tirukkural and Naladiyar stand
foremost. The proverbial saying "nālumiraṇṭumcollukkuuṟuti '' (the
couplets of Tirukkural and the quatrins of Naladiyar are the best of
ethical sayings) glorifies their value in a single sentence. Great
ethical and philosophical utterances have been rendered in poetic form
in Naladiyar. The poetic value of the stanzas cannot be underestimated
although it is true that their moral value pre-eminently appeals to the
readers. W. H. Hudson observes: “there is no reason why poetry should
not be the outcome of philosophy and the vehicle of philosophic truth
without sacrificing anything of its essential poetic qualities and
graces.” Naladiyar belongs to that category of poetry which celebrates
philosophic truth without sacrificing itsessential poetic charm. The
writers of these stanzas were poets who also wished to be great teachers
and moralists.
The authorship of this work is ascribed to a group of Jain ascetics who
lived in the third or fourth century A.D. Its compilation has a tale to
tell. In a critical situation of famine in the Pandiya Country, a group
of eight thousand Jain ascetics left their place each leaving a stanza
behind him. Of them four hundred are sid to have been selected and
compiled. The stanzas themselves testify to the fact that this is not
the work of a single author, but of many belonging to the same school
and age. That is why we find repetition of ideas as in stanzas 207 and
210 and similarity of expression as in stanzas 230 and 238. That this
work is later than Tirukkural is evident from the classification and
arrangement of chapters on the model of the latter, the whole work
consisting of three parts and each chapter contining ten stanzas. But
this classification is not so apt and so complete as in Tirukkural, as
may be seen from the loose connection of the following stanzas to the
chapters in which they are placed :-

The words and ideas expressed in stanza No.305 are not different from
those of the Tirukkural couplets Nos. 1061 and 1069.
This is a Jain work laying great stress on renunciation. The ascetic
note is struck in almost all the chapters except those on High birth
(15), The Support of kindred (21), Scrutiny in forming friendships (22),
Bearing and forbearing in friendship (23), Unreal friend ship (24) and
Chaste matronhood (247). Yet, like Tirukkural, it appeals to the learned
of all religions and sects by virtue of the eternal truths it explins
and the moral philosophy it expounds. There is also a special
effectiveness in elucidating certin ideas in four lines with
appropriate emotional fervour as in stanzas 36, 40 and 83, which is not
so easily possible in the couplets of Tirukkural.
The whole struggle of the human race from barbarism to civilisation is
one continuous effort to mintin and extend its moral dignity. The
learned among the ancient Tamils had perceived this clearly and lid due
emphasis in their teachings on the moral and ethical aspects of human
life. The Tamils are fortunate enough in having preserved these noble
teachings in their ancient language and, as Dr. G. U. Pope has remarked,
“Tirukkural and Naladiyar throw a flood of light upon the whole ethical
and social philosophy of the Tamil people.” Dr. Pope's characteristic
enthusiasm, sincerity and passionate search for truth enabled him to
realise the value of these works and introduce them to the Westerners
through his translations. He carried out his project very successfully.
The grammatical notes, the metrical introduction and the lexicon and
concordance appended to his translation are scholarly and instructive.
His translation has an inspiring quality and is nowhere a travesty of
the original. We are greatly indebted to him for his valuable services
to the advancement of Tamil language and literature.
The South India Saiva Siddhanta Works Publishing Society deserves our
congratulations and thanks for having published this useful translation
of Naladiyarby a renowned scholar and ardent lover of Tamil. F. W.
Ellis' translation of some of the stanzas given at the end of the book
adds to the value of this publication.
Madras-30, 17-2-1958. M. Varadarajan
----------
INTRODUCTION
THIS edition of a popular Tamil Classic was undertaken minly with the
view of assisting Europeans to acquire some acquaintance with classical
Tamil. It is intended to be taken up after the study of the Second
Catechism of Tamil Grammar; and is a companion volume to the Kurral:
these two great works serving as mutual commentaries, and together
throwing a flood of light upon the whole ethical and social philosophy
of the Tamil people.
I am not without a hope and belief that such editions of their great
classics may find favour with some at least of the great multitude of
young Tamil men who pursue with enthusiasm and fair success their
English studies, but are, I apprehend, in some danger, alas ! of
neglecting their own wonderful language.
To avoid repetition, the student is referred to the introduction to the
Kurral for information on many matters connected with Tamil, and
important to those who would thoroughly master the Naladi.
This work is recommended to the student of Tamil for several weighty
reasons.
These are not so much the originality of its teaching, as the peculiar
terseness and vigour of its style ; and the fidelity with which it
reflects the thoughts and ideas of the great mass of the Tamil people,
and indeed of the yeomanry of India ; for, though composed most probably
by Jains, it contins scarcely any traces of their peculiar tenets. For
these the student must read the Jivaga Chintamani, and this is no
easytask, though made easier by the publication of an admirable edition
of it by Mr. Ve. Caminathiyar.
The Naladi-nannurru, or ‘Four Hundred Quatrins,' is often called the
Vellalar-Vetham, the ‘Bible of the Cultivators of the soil.'
Not only is it one of the text-books prescribed for the Oriental Honour
School of this University, but it is moreover a chief subject of
examination in the Madras University; and is tught, in some shape, in
every vernacular school in the Tamil country.
I have therefore in my official capacity prepared under some
disadvantages, this edition, in the hope of bringing at least a few
European minds into closer contact with those of our Tamil
fellow-subjects; and of inducing young Tamil students to bring the freer
and more invigorating, though more laborious methods of European study
to bear upon their own great classics.
A recent Tamil editor of the Naladi begins his preface with these words,
"The Naladiyar is one of the moral text-books emanating from the
(Madura) Academy, (caṅkam) and, therefore, came into existence 4000 years
ago!'
He also gives, with slight variations, the current tradition regarding
the 400 quatrins. It is briefly this : ‘Once on a time, 8000 Jain
ascetics, driven by famine, came to a Pandiyan king and were supported
by him. When the famine was over they prepared to return to their own
country, though the king wished to retin the learned strangers who
added lustre to his court. At last the poets were driven to depart
secretly by night. In the morning it was found that each bard had left
on his seat a quatrin. These were examined and found to differ widely
from one another. The king then ordered them all to be thrown into the
river Vaigi, when it was found that the palm-leaf scrolls contining
these 400 quatrins ascended the river aginst the current, and came to
the bank. To these the king gave the name of Naladiyar (the
quatrinists).' Some other verses reached the banks at various spots,
and are found in two collections, called 'Old sayings' (paḻamoḻi) and ‘The
Essence of the way of virtue' (aṟaneṟiccāram). These atter works have
never obtined the popularity enjoyed by the Naladiyar; and I should
regard them as minly imitations due to a later period, though some of
the verses are certinly more or less ancient.
These traditions point to a collection of popular verses made after the
impalement of the Samanar (or Amanar - Jains, lit, 'naked ones'),
perhaps in the time of Sundara Pandiyan (or Kun-Pandiyan), who probably
lived in the twelfth centuryA.D.
These verses, minly but not, I think, exclusively of Jain origin, were
doubtless expurgated by the Caivas, under whose chief guardianship Tamil
literature has since remined.
They were re-edited, interpreted, arranged, and forced into a kind of
parallelism to the Kurral by Pathumanar (‘a lotus'), of whom nothing is
really known. I have printed an elegant invocation ascribed to him. No
other editor has done much for the quatrins.
The fact that to them exclusively the title of Naladi (or quatrin) was
given seems to render it probable that they constitute some of the
earliest specimens of this elegant metre, the Alcic of Tamil.
As might be expected from their history, no consistent and developed
system of philosophy, religion, or morals can be deduced from them; and
it may be sid with truth that these utterances have too little sequence
and connexion to admit of any scientific criticism. Yet it is a kind of
merit to put a happy point upon it commonplace; and these epigrams,
drawn sometimes from Sanskrit sources and oftener forming the ground of
ornate Sanskrit verses, written in imitation or rivalry, have become
household words through all South India.
I am unwilling to enter here on any discussion of the date of this and
other Tamil classics, since there are scarcely any ascertined facts or
ancient inscriptions from which to reason. The discussion of these
matters requires aptitude, leisure, and opportunity for archeological
research (and these I do not possess), in addition to a critical
acquaintance with Tamil literature. The want of this last essential has
rendered many otherwise profound researches almost valueless. A very
careful consideration of many masterpieces of Tamil literature leads me
to think that between A. D. 800 and 1200 the greatest of these works
were composed. Internal evidence, as far as I see, is all we have to
rely on.
Hiouen-Thsang, the Buddhist pilgrim who visited the continent of India
in 640 A. D., says that in Malakuta the people were not much addicted to
the cultivation of literature, and only valued the pursuit of wealth;
and mentions Jains as the most prominent sect. Now, this Malukuta, must
be Malakotta, Malinadu, Malaya, Malayalam, and seems to have included
the whole Southern part of the Madras Presidency (Hultzsch, South-Indian
Inscriptions, vol, ii part i, p. 2 note) : the Pandya and Cera Kingdoms.
The Jains were great students and copyists of books (Burnell, S. I.
Paleography, p. 88). We may feel quite sure that the seventh century A.
D., at the latest, saw the beginning of Tamil literature under Jain
auspices. Perhaps the Jains fostered the vernaculars partly out of
opposition to the Brahmans. Reformers and missionaries, who generally
address themselves to the intelligent middle classes, have often been
the most assiduous students and promoters of the vulgar tongues.
Quatrin 243 of the Naladishows the feeling of hostility that existed
between North and South : between Hindus and Jains. The great antiquity
of Tamil, which is the one worthy rival of Sanskrit, is abundantly
plin.
The ancient grammatical works existing in Tamil, and its wonderful
metrical system, prove its assiduous cultivation for long ages. An
elaborate, scientific series of metres, such as Tamil glories in,
adapted to every style and theme of composition, is the growth of
centuries. Classical Tamil bears every mark of slow and natural
evolution; but the subject is complicated by the number of works in
circulation falsely attributed to old writers. The many really
ridiculous forgeries of this kind bring discredit on all Tamil
literature. Tamil scholars should banish, among other things, such
spurious works as many of those attributed to Agastya, Avvi,
Pattanattu-pilli, and others.
There is no mention of God in the Naladi (save in the quite modern
invocation) and no trace of religion. In this respect the quatrins
differ from the Kurral. There are indeed a few poetical references to
certin deities, and allusions to popular beliefs and rites, but the
bards evidently were not orthodox Hindus.
The misery of embodiment (piṟavi) in successive and infinitely varied
forms, and the bliss of release (mutti) from all of these, are often
expressed and illustrated with every kind of figure. It is assumed that
always, to all, conscious existence on earth, as well as in any heavens
or hells, is an absolute evil. Here, of course, we find ourselves in
antagonism.
Yet pervading these verses there seems to me to be a strong sense of
moral obligation, an earnest aspiration after righteousness, a fervent
and unselfish charity, and generally a loftiness of aim that are very
impressive. I have felt sometimes as if there must be a blessing in
store for a people that delight so utterly in compositions thus
remarkably expressive of a hunger and thirst after righteousness. They
are the foremost among the peoples of India, and the Kurral and Naladi
have helped to make them so.
It is in vin to discuss elaborately the origin and possible sources of
the work. The majority of the verses were almost certinly sung by Jain
ascetics, yet there are a few that seem to be from the Maha bharata, and
undoubtedly many of the quartrins are fragments of the old ethical
teaching which goes under the name of Niti-Castra. The history of south
India permits us to expect to trace not only Buddhist, or Jain, but also
Greek, Chiristian, and even Muhammadan influences in the early Tamil
classics, as much almost as those inspired by Sanskrit writings. When we
examine each quatrin as an artistic whole kind of cameo--we find that
there are several distinct and clearly marked types. Some of these may
be conveniently arranged in classes.
1. There are the simply didactic.
Here the student must first master the third and fourth lines, in which
is enunciated the truth, precept, or principle, of which the former part
of the quatrin gives the proof or illustration. This is a previling
type also in the Kurral. A Venba verse, like a sonnet, should have a
very effective and striking finish.
This is seen in the first quatrin, where the aphorism is, 'worldly
prosperity is a thing of no account’.
This axiom is illustrated by a matter of dily experience. The
connective is eṉiṉ = 'if this is an undeniable fact, then.......,'
Compare also 293.
2. One class of the didactic quatrin depends for its effectiveness
chiefly on some apt and ingenious simile, illustration, or analogy. To
us some of these seem forced and fanciful conceits, but we really are
not always in a position to judge or rightly estimate them.
In 290 the aptness of the figure, the beauty of the expression, the
wonderful terseness of the conclusion, together with the perfection of
the form and rhythm, leave nothing to be desired.
3. In some of these the simile is simply thrown in, while the most
prominent thing is the clear enunciation of a truth. Here the whole runs
on like rhetorical prose. Examples of this are 8, 49, 87.
4. Some are cumulative, with or without a climax.
Thus in 65 we find three statements, with a thread of connexion : 'It is
the difficulty of the achievement that makes it meritorious.' In 80 we
have three precepts. In 100 there is a climax. See 4, 55, 88.
5. Others are enumerative, where the various parts and accessories of
some idea are summed up. See 74, 81, 83, 34, 119.
6. A few are antithetic, suggesting a contrast with or without a simile;
as, 48, 51, 52, 89.
7. A very few contin puns. So 39, 56.
If I am not deceived there is in many of these verses something far
beyond mere technical skill. At times by a few happy touches an idea is
expressed in such apt language, and illuminated by such a picturesque
use and adaptation of familiar words, each chosen with truest and most
accurate discrimination, that the quatrin becomes a group of life-like
pictures, on which the mind is fain to linger long, and to which it
recurs often. In this matchless verse (135) —

not a syllable could be spared; while almost every word is common and
easy, yet is the very fittest and is used in its exact meaning. It is
somewhat archic ;— has a fascinating air of mystery;—pleasantly
exercises and amply rewards the student's ingenuity ;— seems dark at
first, but once lit up, sparkles for ever.
Thus kari = shore suggests a metaphor: 'learning is the
shoreless—infinite—ocean.'
Then comes the simple antithesis, ‘the learners days are few’. In Tamil
the use of the same root twice (in kalvi and kaṟpavar) and agin in the
third line (kaṟpavē) imparts an added charm.
Into these perfectly (to Tamil ears) harmonious lines is compressed a
whole chapter:
‘The subjects of study (kalvi with a plural verb) are infinitely numerous
; but the learners' days are few : and if it be calmly thought out, men
are liable to many diseases. [piṇi = natural infirmities or “bonds,"
that enfeeble and restrict.] Youthful enthusiasm may lead men to
anticipate great and varied triumphs ; calm reflection teaches them
their natural weakness. So, men. should learn with discrimination
(teḷḷitu), examining closely (ārāy) things befitting (ami, “suit,
satisfy, gladden') them, with intelligence (terintu) like that of the
bird (the semi-divine Hansa), that drinks only the milk and leaves the
water, when these mingled are presented to it. Of course this last is
received as a fact!
I have gratefully to acknowledge the liberal assistance of the Secretary
of State for India in Council as formerly in the case of the Kurral. The
Delegates of the Clarendon Press procured type especially for the book,
and made the arrangements by which this Tamil classic is issued with a
typograpical accuracy hardly ever attined in a similar work. To them my
most grateful thanks are due.
His Highness the Maharaja of Travancore has also subscribed liberally to
the work.
To the skill, patience, and unwearied zeal of Mr. Pembrey, Oriental
reader at the Clarendon Press, it is minly due that no table of errata
is needed to a work so complicated and difficult.
It will be seen that the editor and translator has sought carefully and
patiently to interpret his authors, and has avoided all controversy. His
aim in this work has been simply to introduce Tamil thoughts to English
students. We need to understand one another and love one another, if we
are to be of any mutual benefit. He who knows and loves 'sweet' Tamil
may come to know and love those to whom it is so dear, and thus to be
known and loved by them.
Indian Institute, Oxford,
February, 1893. G. U. Pope.

nālaṭiyār - āṅkila moḻipeyarppu - pōp maṟṟum ellis NALADIYAR --English Translation by Rev. Pope and Mr. Ellis

kaṭavuḷ vāḻttu
(nērici veṇpā)
vāṉoṭu villiṉ varavaṟiyā vāymiyāl
kālnilam tōyāk kaṭavuḷi - yāmnilam
ceṉṉi yuṟavaṇaṅkic cēr tumem uḷḷattu
muṉṉi yavimuṭika eṉṟu.
INVOCATION
Knowing, like bow displayed in heav'n, man's advent here,
To earth my head I bow, to God in truth draw near,
Touch of whose foot earth never knew; thus what my mind
Conceives, I pray may happy consummation find.
This invocation is sid to be by the Commentator Pathumanar.

pakuti 1. aṟattuppāl /PART I. ON VIRTUE
atikāram 1. celva niliyāmi / Ch. 1. The Instability of Prosperity

1.iṉṟu celvarāyiruppavar nāḷip picciyeṭuppavar āvar
aṟucuvi uṇṭi amarntillāḷ ūṭṭa
maṟuciki nīkkiuṇ ṭārum — vaṟiñarāyc
ceṉṟirappar ōriṭattuk kūḻeṉiṉ celvamoṉ
ṟuṇṭāka vikkaṟpāṟ ṟaṉṟu.
Who today dine luxuriously tomorrow beg
Those who ate erewhile, course after course, food of six flavours,
supplied by their complisant spouse, now roam as pupers and beg a mess
of pottage here and there; if so, let wealth be counted as a thing of
nought!
2. celvam niliyātu. pakuttuṇṭu vāḻka.
tukaḷtīr peruñcelvam tōṉṟiyakkāl toṭṭup
pakaṭu naṭantakūḻ pallārō ṭuṇka
akaṭuṟa yārmāṭṭum nillātu celvam
cakaṭakkāl pōla varum.
Wealth abides not, share it and enjoy
When you own ample wealth acquired by blameless means, with many sharing
eat the grin that steers have trodden out! In centre poised prosperity
stands with no man, but revolves like the waggon's wheel.
3. maṉṉarum taṉ nili kulintu māyvar.
yāṉi yeruttam poliyak kuṭiniḻaṟkīḻc
cēṉit talivarāyc ceṉṟōrum — ēṉi
viṉiulappa vēṟāki vīḻvartām koṇṭa
maṉiyāḷi māṟṟār koḷa.
Mighty warriors fall.
Those who rode resplendent forth on the neck of an elephant, beneath the
state umbrella's shade, as the leaders of the host, when 'other deeds'
destroy, shallchange and fall, while foes lead away their wives as
captives.
4.poruḷkaḷ tolikiṉṟaṉa. cāvu varukiṉṟatu.
niṉṟaṉa niṉṟaṉa nillā eṉuṇarn
toṉṟiṉa oṉṟiṉa vallē ceyiṉceyka
ceṉṟaṉa ceṉṟaṉa vāḻnāḷ ceṟuttuṭaṉ
vantatu vantatu kūṟṟu.
Do your duty, knowing the instability of all things.Time flies! Death
comes!
The things of which you sid, ‘they stand, they stand,' stand not; mark
this, and perform what befits, yea! what befits, with all your power!
Your days are gone, are gone! and death close pressing on is come, is
come!
5. muṉṉē aṟañceyka. piṉṉē cāvu varukiṉṟatu.
eṉṉāṉum oṉṟutam kiyuṟap peṟṟakkāṟ
piṉṉāva teṉṟu piṭittirā — muṉṉē
koṭuttār uyappōvar kōṭiltīk kūṟṟam
toṭuttāṟu cellum curam.
Give before death comes.
When you have gined and hold in hand any single thing, retin it not
with the thought, ‘This will serve some other day!’ Those who have given
betimes shall escape the desert road along which death, an unyielding
foe, drags his captives away.
6. iṟattal tiṇṇam. aṟattip purika.
iḻittanāḷ elli ikavā piḻittorīik
kūṟṟam kutittuvantār īṅkilli — āṟṟap
perumporuḷ vittīr vaḻaṅkumiṉ nāḷit
taḻīimtaḻīim taṇṇam paṭum.
Death inevitable. Hoard not !
Man's days pass not their assigned bound. None here on earth have ever
escaped death's power, made off and got free. O hoarder of ample wealth,
dispense it! On the morn the funeral drum will sound.
7.māyvati nīkka makkaḷāl muṭiyātu.
tōṟṟamcāl ñāyiṟu nāḻiyā vikalum
kūṟṟam aḷantunum nāḷuṇṇum — āṟṟa
aṟañcey taruḷuṭiyīr ākumiṉ yārum
piṟantum piṟavātā ril.
Death inevitable.
Death every day takes that fount of light the sun as a measure, metes
out your days, and so devours.Do deeds of virtue full of kindly grace.
Though all are born, none are exempt from death.
8. celvam mēkattu miṉṉalip pōṉṟatu.
celvaryām eṉṟu tām celvuḻi eṇṇāta
pullaṟi vāḷar peruñcelvam — elliṟ
karuṅkoṇmū vāytiṟanta miṉṉuppōl tōṉṟi
maruṅkaṟak keṭṭu viṭum.
The wealth of the foolish like the lightning's flash.
The ample wealth of men of mean understanding, who say, ' We're rich,'
yet ponder not their path and end, appears, and perishes, and leaves no
trace; like the flash, when the black thunder-cloud by night opens its
mouth.
9. īyātavaṉ poruḷ ellām iḻappāṉ
uṇṇāṉ oḷiniṟāṉ ōṅkupukaḻ ceyyāṉ
tuṉṉarum kēḷir tuyarkaḷiyāṉ — koṉṉē
vaḻaṅkāṉ poruḷkāt tiruppāṉēl aā
iḻantāṉeṉ ṟeṇṇap paṭum.
The miser loses all.
He eats not, sheds no light of splendour around, performs no deeds that
merit lofty prise, soothes no sorrow that choice friends feel, spends
nought, but hoards his wealth in vin : “Aha! he's lost it all,' shall
men pronounce.
10. oṉṟum īyātavaṉ tēṉīyi yoppāṉ.
uṭāatum uṇṇātum tamuṭampu ceṟṟum
keṭāata nallaṟamum ceyyār — koṭāatu
vittīṭṭiṉāriḻappar vāṉtōy malināṭa
uyttīṭṭum tēṉīk kari.
The miser like the honey-bee.
Those who stint in clothes and food, and mortify their bodies, yet do
not deeds of deathless virtue, and bestow nothing, hoarding shall suffer
loss:— Lord of the cloud-capped hills !--this the hoarding honey-bee
attests.
---------

2. iḷami niliyāmi / Ch. 2. Youth Abides Not

11.iḷamiyil oḻukkamuṭaṉ vāḻka. mutumi varuvatu uṟuti.
narivarum eṉṟeṇṇi nallaṟi vāḷar
kuḻavi yiṭattē tuṟantār - puritīrā
maṉṉā iḷami makiḻntārē kōlūṉṟi
iṉṉāṅ keḻuntirup pār.
Age will come. Be wise early
"Grey eld will come,’—the wise remembering this renounce the world even
in tender age; but they who joy in youth, unstable, never free from
fault, shall erewhile pinfully rise up leaning on a staff.
12. ellām niliyillātaṉa.
naṭpunār aṟṟaṉa nallārum aḵkiṉār
aṟput taḷiyum aviḻntaṉa — uṭkāṇāy
vāḻtaliṉ ūtiyam eṉṉuṇṭām vantatē
āḻkalat taṉṉa kali.
All is vanity.
Served are the ties of friendship; minished are the pleasant ones;
love's bonds are loosened too; then look within and say, what profit is
there in this joyous life of thine ? The cry comes up as from a sinking
ship!
13. maṉitarkku mūppupparuvam aḻakik keṭukkum.
coltaḷarntu kōlūṉṟic cōrnta naṭiyiṉarāyp
palkaḻaṉṟu paṇṭam paḻikāṟum - ilceṟintu
kāma neṟipaṭarum kaṇṇiṉārk killiyē
ēma neṟipaṭaru māṟu.
Men are loath to give up bodily pleasures.
Speech falters, they lean on a staff, and walk tottering, their teeth
fall out; yet, till the vessel (the body) is scorned by all, they linger
in the house, still indulging fond desires; to these no way of safety
opens out.
14. iḷamiyil num maṉiyāḷ eḻil pārāṭṭiṉīr.
tāḻāt taḷarāt talinaṭuṅkāt taṇṭuṉṟā
vīḻā iṟakkum ivaḷmāṭṭum — kāḻilā
mammarkoḷ māntark kaṇaṅkākun taṉkikkōl
ammaṉikkō lākiya ñāṉṟu.
The cherished wife of your youth.
To men that cherish weak desire for her that's doomed to droop and fail,
supporting her palsied limbs with a staff, and then to fall and pass
away, what anguish comes, when she grasps in her hands the staff her
mother held!
15. talimuṟi talimuṟiyāka iṟappum piṟappum toṭarntu varukiṉṟaṉa.
eṉakkuttāyākiyāḷ eṉṉiīṅ kiṭṭut
taṉakkuttāy nāṭiyē ceṉṟāḷ — taṉakkuttāy
āki yavaḷum atuvāṉāl tāyttāykkoṇ
ṭēkum aḷittiv vulaku.
Endless series of successive generations.
My mother bare me, left me here, and went to seek her mother, who in the
selfsame manner has gone in search; and thus in ceaseless round goes on
the mother-quest. Such is the grace this world affords !
16. paliyiṭum āṭu iliyuṇṭu makiḻvatu pōṉṟatu vāḻvu.
veṟiayar veṅkaḷattu vēlmakaṉ pāṇi
muṟiyār naṟuṅkaṇṇi muṉṉart tayaṅka
maṟikuḷaku uṇṭaṉṉa maṉṉā makiḻcci
aṟivuṭi yāḷarkaṇ il.
The lamb before the sacrificer.
The lamb in the ruddy slughter-house will crop the fragrant shoots that
dangle from the garland in the slayer's hand; such transient gladness of
the thoughtless, youthful hour is never found amid the wise.
17. paḻutta paḻam maṭṭum utirātu. iḷamiyilum maṉitar iṟappar.
paṉipaṭu cōlip payaṉmara mellām
kaṉiutirntu vīḻntaṟ ṟiḷami — naṉiperitum
vēṟkaṇṇaḷ eṉṟivaḷi veḵkaṉmiṉ maṟṟivaḷum
kōṟkaṇṇa ḷākum kuṉintu.
Fruit only ripens to fall. Youth leads to decay.
The sweet fruit from every tree that bears in the dewy grove must fall
to earth. Thus youth decays. Desire not her whose eyes gleam bright as
darts. Full soon she too will walk bent down, with a staff to aid her
dim sight.
18. eṉ uṭal nalam mēlum mēlum māṟivarum. oru niliyillātatu.
paruvam eṉittuḷa palliṉpāl ēṉi
irucikiyum uṇṭīrō eṉṟu—variciyāl
uḷnāṭṭam koḷḷap paṭutalāl yākkikkōḷ
eṇṇār aṟivuṭi yār.
Constant anxieties about health.
’How old are you ?' 'How last your teeth ?' and, ‘Do you eat two courses
yet?' men ask with kindly courtesy. By such close questions urged, the
wise will learn to judge the body as a thing of nought.
19. nāḷik kaṭattātu nalviṉi cey
maṟṟaṟivām nalviṉi yāmiḷiyam eṉṉātu
kittuṇṭām pōḻtē karavā taṟañceymmiṉ
muṟṟi yirunta kaṉiyoḻiyat tīvaḷiyāl
naṟkāy utirtalum uṇṭu.
Aginst Procrastination.
Say not, 'in after time we'll learn virtue, we're young;' but while
wealth is yours conceal it not; do virtuous deeds. When evil tempests
rage, not the ripe fruit alone, but the unripe fruit's fair promise also
falls.
20.tāy alaṟap piḷḷiyik kūṟṟuvaṉ kolkiṟāṉ.
āḷpārt tuḻalum aruḷilkūṟ ṟuṇmiyāl
tōḷkōppuk kālattāl koṇṭuymmiṉ —pīḷpitukkip
piḷḷiyit tāyalaṟak kōṭalāl maṟṟataṉ
kaḷḷam kaṭippiṭittal naṉṟu.
The infant slin by death.
Relentless death is roaming round, and eyes his man! 'Tis true. Take up
your wallet, scape betimes. He bears away the new-born babe, while the
mother sorely laments. It is good to bear in mind his guile.
--------

3. yākki niliyāmi / Ch. 3. The Bodily Frame Endures Not

21. valimiyuṭiya maṉṉarum maṇmēl maṭivār.
malimicit tōṉṟum matiyam pōl yāṉit
talimicik koṇṭa kuṭiyar — nilamicit
tuñciṉār eṉṟeṭuttut tūṟṟappaṭ ṭārallāl
eñciṉār ivvulakat til.
Mighty kings die.
Even kings that rode on elephants beneath the state umbrella's shade,
like the moon appearing over some bill, have had their names proclimed
on earth as dead ;— not any in this world have escaped.
22. vāḻ nāḷ paṟantōṭukiṉṟatu. makkaṭpiṟappiṟkuriya payaṉi nāṭuka.
vāḻnāṭ kalakā vayaṅkoḷi maṇṭilam
vīḻnāḷ paṭāa teḻutalāl — vāḻnāḷ
ulavāmuṉ oppura vāṟṟumiṉ yārum
nilavār nilamici mēl.
Time is fleeting,—use it
As the measure of your days the shining orb each day unfailing rises; so
before your joyous days have passed away, perform ye 'fitting deeds of
grace'; for none abide on earth.
23. ulakil tuṉpamum iṉpamum māṟi māṟi varum.
maṉṟam kaṟaṅka maṇappaṟi yāyiṉa
aṉṟavark kāṅkē piṇappaṟiyāyp — piṉṟi
olittalum uṇṭāmeṉ ṟuyntupōm āṟē
valikkumām māṇṭār maṉam.
Mutability of earthly joys.
The marriage drums that sounded out in the festive hall, there and that
very day have served for him as funeral drums! Men of lofty minds will
note that thus it haps, and will strive to gin the way to escape.
24. iṟantapiṉ paṟi koṭṭum iyalpu.
ceṉṟē eṟipa orukāl ciṟuvari
niṉṟē eṟipa paṟiyiṉi — naṉṟēkāṇ
mukkālik koṭṭiṉuḷ mūṭittīk koṇṭeḻuvar
cettāric cāvār cumantu.
The funeral.
They march and then strike once! A little while they wait, then strike
the drum a second time. Behold, how fine! The third stroke sounds. They
veil it, take the fire, and go forth :—the dying bear the deed!
25. makkaḷ vāḻvil iṉpam illiyeṉṟu cāppaṟi cāṟṟukiṉṟatu.
kaṇam koṇṭu cuṟṟattār kalleṉ ṟalaṟap (ṭīṇ
piṇamkoṇṭu kāṭṭuyppārk kaṇṭum - maṇaṅkoṇ
ṭuṇṭuṇṭuṇ ṭeṉṉum uṇarviṉāṟ cāṟṟumē
ṭoṇṭoṇṭo ṭeṉṉum paṟi.
Death pours contempt on human joys.
To him, who, although he sees them bear the corpse to the burning
ground, while friends in troops loudly lament, boldly asserts that
wedded life is bliss on earth, the funeral drum speaks out, and mocks
his vin utterance.
26. uyir pirintapiṉ uṭampip pōṟṟuvatu aṟiyāmi.
nārttoṭut tīrkkileṉ naṉṟāyn taṭakkileṉ
pārttuḻip peyyileṉ pallōr paḻikkileṉ
tōṟpiyuḷ niṉṟu toḻilaṟac ceytūṭṭum
kūttaṉ puṟappaṭṭak kāl.
The dead body
When the 'soul', that, taking its stand in this skin clad frame, has
fully wrought its works, and partaken of life's experiences, has gone
forth, what matters it whether you attach ropes to the body and drag it
away, or carefully bury it, or throw it aside in any place you light
upon, or if many revile the departed ?
27. nīrkkumiḻipōla nillātu uṭal.
paṭumaḻi mokkuḷil palkālum tōṉṟik
keṭumitōr yākkiyeṉ ṟeṇṇit - taṭumāṟṟam
tīrppēmyām eṉṟuṇarum tiṇṇaṟi vāḷari
nērppāryār nīḷnilattiṉ mēl.
The body a bubble
'Like a bubble, that in pelting rin appears full oft, and disappears,
is this our frame.' So sages have judged, steadfast in wisdom, and have
decided to end this dubious strife. On this wide earth who equal these?
28. maliyiṟ paṭarum mañcupōl maṟiyum uṭal.
yākkiyi yāppuṭittāp peṟṟavar tāmpeṟṟa
yākkiyā lāya payaṉkoḷka — yākki
maliyāṭu mañcupōl tōṉṟimaṟ ṟāṅkē
niliyātu nīttu viṭum.
The body like a cloud on the hillside.
Those who've gined and held fast by this well-knit frame (a human body)
should take the gin the body they have gined is intended to yield.
Like a cloud that wanders over the hills, the body here appears, and
abiding not, departs leaving no trace behind.
29. pulnuṉip paṉipōl uṭal viḻum
pulnuṉimēl nīrpōl niliyāmi eṉṟeṇṇi
iṉṉiṉiyē ceyka aṟaviṉi — iṉṉiṉiyē
niṉṟāṉ iruntāṉ kiṭantāṉtaṉ kēḷalaṟac
ceṉṟāṉ eṉappaṭuta lāl.
The body like dew on the tip of a blade of grass.
Considering that all things are transient as the dew-drop on the tip of
a blade of grass, now, now at once, do virtuous deeds! "Even now he
stood, he sat, he fell—while his kindred cried aloud he died :' such is
man's history!
30. cuṟṟattār cūḻnta toṭarpu niliyaṉṟu.
kēḷātē vantu kiḷikaḷāy iltōṉṟi
vāḷātē pōvarāl māntarkaḷ — vāḷātē
cēkki maraṉoḻiyac cēṇnīṅku puḷpōla
yākki tamarkkoḻiya nīttu.
All human relationships merely temporary.
Unasked men come, appear in the home as kinsmen, and then silently go.
As the bird silently deserts the tree where its nest yet remins, and
goes far off, so these leave but their body to their friends.
-----------

4. aṟaṉvali yuṟuttal / Ch. 4. The Might of Virtue

31. piccikkārar piṟarvācal niṟpār
akattārē vāḻvāreṉ ṟaṇṇāntu nōkkip
pukattām peṟāar puṟaṅkaṭi paṟṟi
mikattām varunti iruppārē mēlit
tavattāl tavañceyyā tār.
The door closed. Too late.
‘Yet those within are blest,' so saying, they look up, but obtin no
entrance: their place is at the outer gate. There will they suffer much,
who thro' lack of former penitence do no penance now. [In a former state
penance won for them a human shape. As men they have now failed.]
32. vāḻnāḷ kaḻiyum. varunti aṟam cey.
āvānām ākkam nacii aṟammaṟantu
pōvā nām eṉṉāp pulineñcē — ōvātu
niṉṟuñaṟṟi vāḻti eṉiṉumniṉ vāḻnāḷkaḷ
ceṉṟaṉa ceyva turi.
What wilt thou do in the end thereof?
Say not, O silly soul, we will live desiring wealth and die forgetting
virtue! We'll say that ceaselessly toiling thou shalt live long; but
tell me, what wilt thou do when all thy happy days are over?
33. viṉippayaṉ tuṉpamāy viḷivatu eṉṟaṟika.
viṉippayaṉ vantakkāl veyya uyirā
maṉattiṉ aḻiyumām pēti - niṉittataṉit
tolliya teṉṟuṇar vārē taṭumāṟṟat
telli ikantoruvu vār.
The wise accept the sorrows of life as retributive.
When the ‘fruit of deeds' is come, the fool sighs heavily, and all his
soul dies out; but those who reflect and say, ,'Tis old desert,' will
pass beyond the bound of life's perplexity, and escape (by devoting
themselves to virtue).
34. piṟappiṉ payaṉ aṟattip purivatē.
arumpeṟal yākkiyip peṟṟa payattāl
perumpayaṉum āṟṟavē koḷka — karumpūrnta
cāṟupōl cālavum piṉutavi maṟṟataṉ
kōtupōl pōkum uṭampu.
Virtue is the gin. The body mere refuse.
As the gin from the mortal frame now reached —and which is so hard to
reach —with all thy might lay hold of virtue's lasting good. As the
juice expressed from the sugar-cane 'twill afterwards be thine aid, when
the body goes like refuse flung away.
35. aṟappayaṉ aṭintōr iṟappataṟku añcār.
karumpāṭṭik kaṭṭi ciṟukālik koṇṭār
turumpeḻuntu vēmkāl tuyarāṇ ṭuḻavār
varunti uṭampiṉ payaṉkoṇṭār kūṟṟam
varuṅkāl pariva tilar.
The same. The body only sapless stalks.
Those who have pressed the sugar-cane, and early taken the juice, when
the refuse heaped up burns, will suffer no grief: those who have toiled
and gined the fruit won from embodied existence will feel no pangs when
death shall come.
36. cāvu neruṅkiyatu tarmam purika.
iṉṟukol aṉṟukol eṉṟukol eṉṉātu
piṉṟiyē niṉṟatu kūṟṟameṉ ṟeṇṇi
oruvumiṉ tīyavi ollum vakiyāl
maruvumiṉ māṇṭār aṟam.
Death stands waiting behind you. Delay not!
‘This day ?’ That day? What day? O question not the time! Bethink you
death stands behind you ever waiting! Put from you every evil thing; and
with all your powers embrace the virtue which sages teach.
37. uyirkaṭkuṟuti nalviṉi oṉṟē.
makkaḷā lāya perumpayaṉum āyuṅkāl
ettuṇiyum āṟṟap palavāṉāl — tokka
uṭampiṟkē oppuravu ceytoḻukā tumpark
kiṭantuṇṇap paṇṇap paṭum.
Use the body to gin the world to come.
When you examine closely the mighty gins to be acquired by birth in a
human shape, if they seem manifold, perform not deeds which suit the
body's frame alone, but deeds whose fruit is joy in the world to come!
38. takkārkku utavuvatu mikka payaṉ viḷikkum.
uṟakkum tuṇiyatōr ālamvit tīṇṭi
iṟappa niḻalpayan tāaṅku — aṟappayaṉum
tāṉ ciṟitāyiṉum takkārkip paṭṭakkāl
vāṉciṟitāp pōrttu viṭum.
A benefit conferred by the worthy on the worthy.
The banyan seed, though it be minute as one might see in dreams, grows
to a mighty tree of amplest shade; so gifts from a virtuous hand,
received by a worthy hand, though small, will hide the diminish'd
heavens.
39. vāḻnāḷ kaḻivatu kaṇṭu varuntuka.
vikalum vikal varakkaṇṭum aḵtuṇarār
vikalum vikali vikumeṉ ṟiṉpuṟuvar
vikalum vikaltam vāḻnāḷmēl vittal
vikali vittuṇarā tār.
Days pass.
Daily they see the passing day added to the sum of the days gone by, as
a day that is spent from out the store of their days, yet dily, as they
see day dawn, they say joyously, 'This day will abide with us till the
close of day?’
40. irantuyir vāḻvati iḻivueṉa viṭuka.
māṉa aruṅkalam nīkki iraveṉṉum
īṉa iḷiviṉāl vāḻvēṉmaṉ — īṉattāl
ūṭṭiyak kaṇṇum uṟuticērn tivvuṭampu
nīṭṭittu niṟkum eṉiṉ.
Why should man mintin the perishable body by dishonourable begging ?
Parting with honour's jewel I might still consent to live a suppliant's
life of shame, if when mintined by such disgrace, this body could
abide in strength and last for length of days.
--------------

5. tūytaṉmi / Ch. 5. Impurity

41. mēṟṟōl nīṅkiṉāl nāṟṟamām uṭampu.
mākkēḻ maṭanallāy eṉṟaraṟṟum cāṉṟavar
nōkkārkol noyyatōr pukkili[#]— yākkikkōr
īcciṟa kaṉṉatōr tōlaṟiṉum vēṇṭumē
kākki kaṭivatōr kōl.
Any slight wound may fester, and reduce the fairest form to a loathsome
state.
‘O gentle miden, fair and good !' These paragons that thus rave, know
they not 'the heavenly home invisible?’ Let a bit of skin be broken
slight as an insect's wing, and you need the stick that drives a way the
crows !
[#] (pāṭam) tuccili.
42. mātar uṭal kaṇṭu mayaṅkal ākātu.
tōṟpōrvi mēlum toḷipalavāyp poymmaṟikku
mīppōrvi māṭcit tuṭampāṉāl — mīppōrvi
poymmaṟiyāk kāmam pukalātu maṟṟataṉip
pimmaṟiyāp pārkkap paṭum.
The body's beauty only skin - deep.
If the body which, with a covering of skin above, possesses many
apertures, owes its beauty only to that outward cloak that veils the
false (the inner foulness), then is it fitting to say no word of lustful
desire which hides the false from itself by the covering veil, but to
regard that body as an undeveloped embryo.
43. oppaṉiyāl uṭal aḻukku nīṅkātu.
takkōlam tiṉṟu tali niṟiyap pūccūṭip
poykkōlam ceyya oḻiyumē — ekkālum
uṇṭi viṉiyuḷ uṟikkum eṉapperiyōr
kaṇṭuki viṭṭa mayal.
Outward adornment is not inward purification.
Will impurity ever cease from the worthless body which the great have
abandoned, knowing it to be reeking with odours from processes connected
with nutrition, though aromatics be chewed, the head covered with
garlands, and the body adornedwith false splendour?
44. kaṟṟōr peṇkaḷiṉ kaṇkaṇṭu mayaṅkār
teṇṇīrk kuvaḷi porukayal vēleṉṟu
kaṇṇilpuṉ mākkaḷ kavaṟṟa viṭuvēṉō
uṇṇīr kaḷintakkāl nuṅkucūṉ ṟiṭṭaṉṉa
kaṇṇīrmi kaṇṭoḻuku vēṉ.
Female eyes shall not bewilder me !
Shall I abandon (my ascetic purpose) becuse blind, low men worry me,
saying (that woman's eye is like) the water-lily in the clear stream, or
the warring carp, or a javelin? I will pursue my virtuous way as having
seen (that) the eye's real nature (is), like (that of) the palm-tree
fruit (which is) scooped out, after the water has been drined off!
45. maṅkiyar palleḻilil mayaṅkuvar aṟivilār.
mulli mukimuṟuval mutteṉ ṟivipitaṟṟum
kallāppuṉ mākkaḷ kavaṟṟa viṭuvēṉō
ellāruṅ kāṇap puṟaṅkāṭ ṭutirntukka
palleṉpu kaṇṭoḻuku vēṉ.
The beauty of women's teeth shall not bewilder me.
Though worthless men untught should fret my soul and rave of teeth like
jasmine buds and pearls, shall I forego my fixed resolve, who have seen
in the burning ground those bones—the fallen teeth —strewn round for all
to see ?
46. kuṭarmutal uṟuppukkaḷ kūṭiyatu uṭampu.
kuṭarum koḻuvum kurutiyum eṉpun
toṭarum narampoṭu tōlum — iṭiyiṭiyē
vitta taṭiyum valampumām maṟṟivaṟṟuḷ
ettiṟattāḷ īrṅkōti yāḷ.
'This vile body.'
(The body) is entrils, and marrow, and blood, and bone, and connecting
tendons, and skin, and here and there flesh interposed, and fat. In the
midst of these, what sort of a being is she who wears the fresh
garlands?
47. maṅkiyar mēl aḻaku miyali viḷikkum.
ūṟi uvarttakka oṉpatu vāyppulaṉum
kōtik kuḻampalikkum kumpattip —pēti
peruntōḷi peyvaḷāy eṉṉumīp pōrtta
karuntōlāl kaṇviḷakkap paṭṭu.
The body disgusting.
The fool will address the earthen pot (the body), from which defilement
oozes, which from nine disgusting outlets scatters pollution, and in
which slimy liquids move to and fro—and say, 'O thou of the rounded
arms,' ‘O thou with armlets decked, ‘—becuse it is made bright to his
eyes by a covering of black skin.
48. cantaṉam pūuṭal cārttiṉum payaṉili.
paṇṭam aṟiyār paṭucāntum kōtiyum
[#]koṇṭupārāṭṭuvār kaṇṭilarkol - maṇṭip
peṭiccēval vaṉkaḻuku pērttiṭṭuk kuttum[§]
muṭiccākā ṭacciṟ ṟuḻi .
The body a prey to corruption.
They know not what the body is; with sandal paste and flowers they make
it fine. Have they not seen, I pray, the vultures and their mates in
flocks with busy beaks devour the body foul when the chariot-axle is
snapt?
[#] (pāṭam) kaṇṭu. [§]kuttaṉ.
49. vāḻviṉ iḻivi maṇṭiyōṭu kāṭṭum.
kaḻintār iṭutali kaṇṭārneñ cuṭkak
kuḻintāḻnta kaṇṇavāyt tōṉṟi — oḻintārip
pōṟṟi neṟiniṉmiṉ iṟṟitaṉ paṇpeṉṟu
cāṟṟuṅkol cālac cirittu.
The eyeless skulls teach.
The skulls of the dead, at the sight of which the gazer fears, with deep
cavernous eyes appear, and grinning say to those who still survive,
'Guard well! In virtue's path stand fast. This is the body's grace and
worth.''
50. iṭukāṭṭu veṇṭali nam iṟumāppu nīkkum
uyirpōyār veṇṭali uṭkac cirittuc
ceyirtīrkkum cemmāp pavaric — ceyirtīrntār
kaṇṭiṟ ṟitaṉvaṇṇam eṉpataṉāl tammiyōr
paṇṭattuḷ vippatilar.
The sight of the skulls cures pride.
The skulls of the dead, grinning so as to excite disgust, cure the vin
lovers of life of their folly. Those who are cured of this folly, seeing
(the skulls in the burning ground), say “such is this body,' and so
value themselves as nothing.
--------------

6. tuṟavu / Ch. 6. Renunciation

51. tavattiṉmuṉ pāvam nillātu.
viḷakkup pukiruḷ māyntāṅ koruvaṉ
tavattiṉmuṉ nillātām pāvam — viḷakku ney
tēyviṭattuc ceṉṟiruḷ pāyntāṅku nalviṉi
tīrviṭattu niṟkumām tītu.
Penitence puts sin to flight.
As when a lamp enters darkness dies, so sin stands not before man's
penitence. As, when in a lamp the oil wastes, darkness rushes in, so
evil takes its stand where deeds of virtue cease.
52. kaṇitam ilakkaṇam kaṟpatiṟ payaṉilli. kaṭami ceyka.
niliyāmi nōymūppuc cākkāṭeṉ ṟeṇṇit
taliyāyār taṅkarumañ ceyvār — tolivillāc
cattamum cōtiṭamum eṉṟāṅ kivipitaṟṟum
pittariṉ pētiyār il.
Put away useless studies.
The chief of men reflect on change, disease, old age, and death, and do
their needful work. Those who raving teach the endless round of
sevenfold science, and the lore of stars, are maddest of the foolish
throng.
53. ellāp poruḷkaḷum nillā vulakil.
illam iḷami eḻilvaṉappu mīkkūṟṟam
celvam valieṉ ṟiviyellām — mella
niliyāmi kaṇṭu neṭiyār tuṟappar
taliyāyār tāmuyyak koṇṭu.
Renunciation at once.
The chief of men in quite thought discern how house, and youth, and
beauty's grace, and high estate, and wealth, and strength, all pass
away; and thus, to save themselves, prolonging not the time, renounce
all these.
54. tuṉpavāḻvu ituveṉṟu tuṟappar mēlōr.
tuṉpam palanāḷ uḻantum orunāḷi
iṉpamē kāmuṟuvar ēḻiyār — iṉpam
iṭiterin tiṉṉāmi nōkki maṉiyā
ṟaṭivoḻintār āṉṟamin tār.
Pleasure and pin.
Though wretched men suffer afflictions many a day, yet one day's delight
they eagerly desire. The men of calm and full wisdom, in pleasure's core
see pin, and quit the pleasant household paths.
55. mūppunōy varuvataṉ muṉtuṟavu pēṇuka.
koṉṉē kaḻintaṉ ṟiḷamiyum iṉṉē
piṇiyoṭu mūppum varumāl — tuṇivoṉṟi
eṉṉoṭu cūḻā teḻuneñcē pōtiyō
naṉṉeṟi cēra namakku.
Unreflecting soul, why not seek the way of peace?
In vin is my youth spent. Even now disease and old age will come. O
soul ! be bold; wrangle no more with me, but rise! Wilt thou not go
where both thou and I may gin virtue's path?
56. vaṟumi kuṟittēṉum tuṟavu koḷka .
māṇṭa kuṇattōṭu makkaṭpēṟilleṉiṉum
pūṇṭāṉ kaḻittaṟ karumiyāl — pūṇṭa
miṭieṉṉum kāraṇattiṉ mēṉmuṟik kaṇṇē
kaṭieṉṟār kaṟṟaṟin tār.
Marriage to be dreaded.
Though your wife possess no excellence and bear no child, it is hard to
get rid of the marriage bond. For this cuse since he who weds puts
sorrow on, in olden days the learned made marriage a synonym of dread.
57. tuṉpam poṟuttut tuṟavu mēṟkoḷka.
ūkkittām koṇṭa virataṅkaḷ uḷḷuṭiyat
tākkarun tuṉpaṅkaḷ tāmtali vantakkāl
nīkki niṟūum uravōrē nalloḻukkam
kākkum tiruvat tavar.
Patience and perseverance.
When troubles arise, hard to resist, to cuse them to break the vows
which their lofty spirits have pledged, the men of power set griefs
aside and firmly fix their souls in right. These are the blessed,
guarding decorum's rule.
58. tammi ikaḻntālum poṟuppatutāṉ tavam.
tammi ikaḻntami tāmpōṟuppa taṉṟimaṟ
ṟemmi ikaḻnta viṉippayattāl — ummi
erivāy nirayattu vīḻvarkol eṉṟu
parivatūum cāṉṟōr kaṭaṉ.
Forbearance and pity for evil-doers.
To bear with those that speak contemptuous words; yea, more, to say,
'Ah, will these sink in the other world to hell, the place of fire, as
fruit of their contemptuous words;' and to grieve, is duty of the
perfect man.
59. aimpulaṉ aṭakkiṉōr muttiyi yaṭivār.
meyvāykaṇ mūkkuc ceviyeṉap pērpeṟṟa
aivāya vēṭki avāviṉik — kivāy
kalaṅkāmaṟ kāttuykkum āṟṟaluṭiyāṉ
vilaṅkātu vīṭu peṟum.
Repression of sensuous emotions.
He who undisturbed, in the ordered way of right, has power to guard and
guide the desires and lusts that find entrance by the five sense-gates,
called 'body, mouth, eye, nose, and ear' - unfailing shall gin
'release.'
60. tuṉpaṅ kaṇṭu tuṟappār mēlōr.
tuṉpamē mītūrak kaṇṭum tuṟavuḷḷār
iṉpamē kāmuṟuvar ēḻiyār — iṉmam
icitoṟum maṟṟataṉ iṉṉāmi nōkkip
pacital pariyātām mēl.
The bitter pleasures of life.
Though wretched men behold afflictions urge and press, renunciation is
not in their thoughts; delight they eagerly desire. The great in every
joy behold its pin, and seek it not.
-----

7. ciṉamiṉmi / Ch. 7. The Absence of Anger : Meekness

61. matittālum ikaḻntālum maṉaṅkoḷḷār mēlōr.
matittiṟap pārum iṟakka matiyā
mitittiṟap pārum iṟakka - mitittēṟi
īyum talimēl iruttalāl aḵtaṟivār
kāyum katamiṉmi naṉṟu.
Disregard of the esteem or disesteem of men.
Who pass esteeming us, let them pass on! And those who contemn and
trample on us as they pass, let them too pass on! If even a fly
(especially unclean) should climb, trampling on their head, it is well
that the wise who know its worth, should feel no wrath.
62. kōpam variṉum poṟumi koḷka.
taṇṭāc ciṟappiṉtam iṉṉuyirit tāṅkātu
kaṇṭuḻi yellām tuṟappavō — maṇṭi
aṭipeyarā tāṟṟa iḷivanta pōḻtiṉ
muṭikiṟkum uḷḷat tavar.
Resolute men bear meekly the evils of life.
Although disgraces throng thickly, and may not be repulsed, will those
whose minds are set upon finishing the work begun, renounce sweet life's
unfailing worth in their impatience, whenever they see (evils)?
63. paḻitturippōriyum paḻiyār aṟiñar.
kāvā toruvaṉtaṉ vāytiṟantu collumcol
ōvātē taṉṉic cuṭutalāl –- ōvātē
āyntaminta kēḷvi aṟivuṭiyār eññāṉṟum
kāyntaminta collār kaṟuttu.
Rashness in speech hurts one's self.
If a man open his mouth and speak unguarded words, his words will
ceaselessly burn his soul. The wise who ceaselessly hear, and ponder
well and calmly, even in their wrath, will never give utterance to words
of fire.
64. kīḻmakkaḷ kōpam mēṉmēṟ kiḷarntu eḻum.
nērttu nikarallār nīralla colliyakkāl
vērttu vekuḷār viḻumiyōr - ōrttataṉi
uḷḷattāṉ uḷḷi uritturāy ūrkēṭpat
tuḷḷittūṇ muṭṭumām kīḻ.
The good man's meekness. The low man's ungoverned fury.
When men who are beneath them confront them, and speak unseemly words,
the excellent wax not hot with anger. The base man will brood over it,
chafe and rave for all the town to hear, and leap, and dash his head
aginst a post.
65. iḷiyāṉ aṭakkam, illāṉ koṭi, valiyavaṉ poṟumi mūṉṟum ciṟantaṉa.
iḷiyāṉ aṭakkam aṭakkam kiḷiporuḷ
illāṉ koṭiyē koṭippayaṉ — ellām
oṟukkum matuki uraṉuṭi yāḷaṉ
poṟukkum poṟiyē poṟi.
Self-restrint in youth, gifts from the poor, and forbearance in the
mighty are excellent.
The young man's self-restrint is self-restrint. The gift of him who
owns no stores of wealth is gift indeed. When wan has means and might to
punish every fault, if he forbear, call him the patient man.
66. kīḻmakkaḷ paḻippiṉum mēṉmakkaḷ poṟuppar
kaleṟin taṉṉa kayavarvāy iṉṉāccol
ellāruṅ kāṇap poṟuttuyppar — olli
iṭunīṟṟāl piavinta nākampōl tattam
kuṭimiyāṉ vātikkap paṭṭu.
Noble instinct restrins.
As stones the base shower down their bitter words: the noble bear, in
sight of all, and let them pass, by sense of noble worth constrined :
like serpent's crest at once by touch of sacred ash subdued.
67. piṟarkkut tuṉpam puriyār periyār.
māṟṟārāy niṉṟutam māṟēṟpārk kēlāmi
āṟṟāmi eṉṉār aṟivuṭiyār — āṟṟāmi
nērttiṉṉā maṟṟavar ceytakkāl tāmavarip
pērttiṉṉā ceyyāmi naṉṟu.
Return not evil for evil.
When men stand forth as our enemies, and would begin the conflict, to
decline the strife is not, in the language of the wise, lack of power.
Even when men have confronted and done us intolerable evils, it is good
not to do them evil in return.
68. cāṉṟōr kōpam tāṉē taṇiyum.
neṭuṅkālam ōṭiṉum nīcar vekuḷi
keṭuṅkālam iṉṟip pākkum — aṭuṅkāli
nīrkoṇṭa veppampōl tāṉē taṇiyumē
cīrkoṉṭa cāṉṟōr ciṉam.
The wrath of the base never expends itself. That of the good of itself
dies out. Long time though base men's wrath run on, it spreads abroad,
and knows no time when heat is spent; as the heat of water, when boiled
at cooking time, itself grow's cool, the ire of perfect men of worth
abates.
69. naṟkuṭip piṟantōr naṉmiyē ceyvār.
upakāram ceytataṉi ōrātē taṅkaṇ
apakāram āṟṟar ceyiṉum — upakāram
tāñceyva tallāl tavaṟṟiṉāl tīṅkūkkal
vāṉtōy kuṭippiṟantārk kil.
It is not the way of the noble to do evil to those who injure them.
Though men think not of good received, and do much ill to men of family
whose fame has touched the sky, these still do good; nor are they wont
provoked by faults, to render evil to the thankless ones.
70. mēṉmakkaḷ vāyil tāḻntacol veḷippaṭā.
kūrttunāy kuvik koḷakkaṇṭum tamvāyāl
pērttunāy kuviṉār īṅkilli — nīrttaṉṟik
kīḻmakkaḷ kīḻāy colliyakkāl colpavō
mēṉmakkaḷ tamavāyāl mīṭṭu.
Return not riling for riling.
Though a dog, in range, should lay hold of them, there are none in the
world who, in return, would lay hold of it with their mouth; and thus,
when low men speak, not what is fitting, but low words, will high-minded
men, in reply, utter such words with their mouth.
----------

8. poṟiyuṭimi / Ch. 8. Patience

71. aṟivilārōṭu uriyāṭutal tītu.
kōti yaruvik kuḷirvari naṉṉāṭa!
pētiyō ṭiyātum uriyaṟka — pēti
urippiṟ citinturikkum ollum vakiyāṉ
vaḻukkik kaḻitalē naṉṟu.
Don't argue with the foolish.
Lord of the pleasant land, where down the cool mountins the streams
fall as garlands!— With a fool hold no converse. If you speak with him,
in replying he will pervert your words. To slip away from him as best
you can is well.
72. pollāruriyiyum poṟuppatē perumi.
nērallār nīralla colliyakkāl maṟṟatu
tārittiruttal takutimaṟ —ṟōrum
pukaḻmiyāk koḷḷātu poṅkunīr ñālam
camaḻmiyāk koṇṭu viṭum.
Insults from inferiors.
When persons not our equals say unfitting things, to bear and to be
still is worthy conduct. The world girt by foaming waters regards not a
contrary course as prise-worthy, but as discreditable.
73. aṉpar kaṭuñcollum naṉmi payakkum
kātalār colluṅ kaṭuñcol uvanturikkum
ētilār iṉcoliṉ tītāmō — pōtelām
mātarvaṇ ṭārkkum malikaṭal taṇcērppa!
āva taṟivārp peṟiṉ.
Harsh words of those who love are better than complisant words of foes.
Lord of the swelling sea's cool shore, where bright insects hum around
every flower!—Are severe words from loving lips harder to bear, if men
can only rightly estimate their result, than pleasant words that
strangers courteously speak?
74. naṉmi ceyvōṉ ennāḷum makiḻvāṉ.
aṟiva taṟintaṭaṅki añcuva tañci
uṟuva tulakuvappac ceytu — peṟuvataṉāl
iṉpuṟṟu vāḻum iyalpiṉār eññāṉṟun
tuṉpuṟṟu vāḻtal aritu.
The thoroughly disciplined and contented man is happy.
Those who know what should be known and rule themselves thereby; who
fear what should be feared ; who use all their faculties to bless the
world; and whose nature rejoices in all good gined: are for ever free
from woes.
75. vēṟṟumi naṭpit tūṟṟātu viṭuka.
vēṟṟumi yiṉṟik kalantiruvar naṭṭakkāl
tēṟṟā voḻukkam oruvaṉkaṇ uṇṭāyiṉ
āṟṟun tuṇiyum poṟukka ; poṟāṉāyiṉ
tūṟṟātē tūra viṭal.
If a friend act doubtfully, forgive or quietly withdraw.
When two with strict accord unite in friendship's bond, if one betray
the other's confidence by unkind act, this latter should endure as best
he may. And if he can't endure, he should not divulge it, but withdraw
himself.
76. tuṉpam ceyyiṉum naṇpaṉi viṭātē.
iṉṉā ceyiṉum iṉiya oḻikeṉṟu
taṉṉiyē tāṉnōviṉ allatu — tuṉṉik
kalantārik kiviṭutal, kāṉaka nāṭa!
vilaṅkiṟkum viḷḷal aritu.
If a friend do evil to you, think it good, refrin from anger and blame
yourself ;
but never forsake him.
Lord of the woodlands! Separation is hard even to beasts; therefore if
friends do things that are unpleasant, think them pleasant. bid yourself
cease (from wrath), and blame yourself alone; but forsake not those that
have been joined to you in the intimacy of friendship.
77. periyōr naṭpiṟ piḻi poṟuppavarē.
periyār perunaṭpuk kōṭaltām ceyta
ariya poṟuppeṉ ṟaṉṟō — ariyarō
oleṉ aruvi uyarvari naṉṉāṭa!
nallacey vārkkut tamar.
Forbearance cements friendship.
Is not the reason why the close friendship of the great is sought, that
they will bear even with faults hard to endure? Lord of the good land of
high mountins, with resounding waterfalls! — to good people are
intimate friends rare?
78. kuṟi nīkkuvōriṭam kuṟipāṭu kūṟuka.
vaṟṟimaṟ ṟāṟṟap pacippiṉum paṇpilārk
kaṟṟam aṟiya uriyaṟka - aṟṟam
maṟikkun tuṇiyārk kurippavē tammit
tuṟakkun tuṇivilā tār.
Bear want
Though sore wasted with hunger, let not men tell out their destitution
to ungracious churls. Those indeed who lack resolution to deny
themselves may tell their wants to those who are able and willing to
save them from destitution.
79. iṉpamē variṉum iḻiceyal oḻikka.
iṉpam payantāṅ kiḻivu talivariṉum
iṉpattiṉ pakkam iruntikku —iṉpam
oḻiyāmi kaṇṭālum ōṅkaruvi nāṭa!
paḻiyākā āṟē tali.
Forsake pleasure that brings disgrace. To avoid guilt is the chief
matter.
Although shameful things may present themselves. as things that yield
pleasure, flee from that pleasure's side! Though thou couldst see
pleasures that cease not soon, — Lord of the land of fountins high! —
the guiltless way is best.
80. poyyuri, piṟarkkuk kēṭu ceytal, takavilār iṭattu uṇṇal ceyyat takātaṉa.
tāṉkeṭiṉum takkārkē ṭeṇṇaṟka taṉuṭampiṉ
ūṉkeṭiṉum uṇṇārkit tuṇṇaṟka — vāṉkavinta
viyakam ellām peṟiṉum uriyaṟka
poyyō ṭiṭimiṭinta col.
Never desire evil, nor eat with improper persons, nor lie.
Though ruin seize you plan not ruin to the just! Though body's flesh
should waste, eat not from hands unfit! Though the whole earth
o'er-arched by heaven accrue as gin, never speak word with falsehood
mixed !

9. piṟarmaṉi nayavāmi / Ch. 9. The Not Desiring Other Men's Wives

81. ayalār maṉiviyi aṟiñar virumpār.
accam peritāl, ataṟkiṉpam ciṟṟaḷavāl
niccam niṉiyuṅkāṟ kōkkoliyāl, —niccalum
kumpikkē kūrtta viṉiyāl piṟaṉtāram
nampaṟka nāṇuṭi yār.
Aginst adultery.
The fear it brings is great ; its pleasure is brief ; each day if it is
divulged death threatens by the king's decree; and ever it is a deed
that tends to the pins of hell; O shamefast men, desire not your
neighbour's wife.
82. ayal maṉi virumpiṉāl accam paki paḻi pāvam cērum.
aṟampukaḻ kēṇmi perumiin nāṉkum
piṟaṉ tāram naccuvārc cērā — piṟaṉtāram
naccuvārc cērum pakipaḻi pāvameṉ
ṟaccattō ṭinnāṟ poruḷ.
The sinner forfeits much, and incurs much.
Virtue, prise, friendship, greatness, all these four draw not anigh the
men who covet their neighbour's wife. Hatred, disgrace and guilt, with
fear, these four possessions abide with men who covet their neighbour's
wife.
83. ayalmaṉi viruppam eppōtum accam ām.
pukka viṭattaccam pōtarum pōtaccam
tuykku miṭattaccam tōṉṟāmaṟ kāppaccam
ekkālum accam tarumāl evaṉkolō
uṭkāṉ piṟaṉil pukal.
Fear on every side.
In entering in there's fear; in home returning fear; during enjoyment is
fear; in guarding the secret is fear; it evermore brings fear: why shuns
he not with dread the entrance of neighbour's house?
84. piṟarmaṉi virumpiṉāl peruntuṉpam viḷiyum.
kāṇiṉ kuṭippaḻiyām kiyuṟiṉ kālkuṟiyum
āṇiṉmi ceyyuṅkāl accamām — nīḷnirayat
tuṉpam payakkumāl; tuccāri! nī kaṇṭa
iṉpam eṉakkeṉittāl kūṟu.
The way of transgressors is hard.
If any one see, disgrace lights on the house; if any hand should seize,
leg's mimed; in the doing of the shameful deed is dread; it yields as
fruit vast hell's affliction : tell me, O profligate, what measure of
delight is thine ?
85. piṟarmaṉi viḻintōr pēṭiyāyp piṟappar.
cemmiyoṉ ṟiṉṟic ciṟiyā riṉattarāyk
kommi varimuliyāḷ tōḷmarīi - ummi
valiyāṟ piṟarmaṉimēṟ ceṉṟārē immi
aliyāki āṭiuṇ pār.
Punishment of ravishers.
Those who, in a former state, without any regard to right, becoming
associates of the mean, enjoyed the embraces of beautiful women, and by
violence approached their neighbour's wife, in this state will become
eunuchs, and dancing shall earn their bread.
86. ayalmaṉi virumpuvatu nallaṟam ārō?
pallār aṟiyap paṟiyaṟintu nāḷkēṭṭuk
kalyāṇam ceytu kaṭippukka— melliyal
kātal maṉiyāḷum illāḷā eṉoruvaṉ
ētil maṉiyāḷi nōkku.
Why should a man who has his own wife look at his neighbour's ?
While his loving wife dwells in his home, the tender one whom he
espoused, — seeking (out an auspicious) day, and sounding the drum, for
many folks to know,—and 'whom he guards as his own, what means a man's
glance at another's wife?
87. piṟarmaṉi virumputal pētiyiṉ ceyalē.
ampal ayaleṭuppa añcit tamarparīi
vampalaṉ peṇmarīimintuṟṟu — nampum
nilimiil neñcattāṉ tuppuravu pāmpiṉ
talinakki yaṉṉa tuṭittu.
Pleasure dearly purchased.
The enjoyment of the man of unstable heart, who under the influence of
infatuation approaches his neighbour's wife and sets his affections upon
her, while neighbours spread abroad his guilt, and kinsmen dread and
mourn, is like (that of the person who takes pleasure in) licking a
serpent's head.
88. aṟiñar kāmam akattuḷ aṭaṅkum.
paravā veḷippaṭā pallōrkaṇ taṅkā
uravōrkaṇ kāmanōy ōō koṭitē!
viravāruḷ nāṇup paṭalañci yātum
uriyātuḷ āṟi viṭum.
The passions of virtuous men are under control.
The disease of lust in men mighty (in wisdom) gins not ascendancy, is
not revealed, does not remin fixed on many objects.
O! it is a cruel conflict; but fearing to incur shame in the midst of
their foes, they say nothing about it, and it is extinguished within
them.
89. akam cuṭum kāmam ataṉāṟ koṭiyatu.
ampum aḻalum avirkatir ñāyiṟum
vempic cuṭiṉum puṟamcuṭum — vempik
kavaṟṟi maṉattic cuṭutalāṟ kāmam
avaṟṟiṉum añcap paṭum.
Lust the most deadly enemy.
Arrow and fire and sun with glistening rays may rage and burn; but these
burn the outer man alone. Lust rages and distracts and burns the mind,
and is more to be feared than they.
90.tīyiṉum koṭiyatu kāmam eṉṟu eṇṇuka.
ūruḷ eḻunta urukeḻu centīkku
nīruḷ kuḷittum uyalākum — nīruḷ
kuḷippiṉum kāmam cuṭumēkuṉṟēṟi
oḷippiṉum kāmam cuṭum.
Fire and lust.
From the ruddy fire that fiercely rises in the village you may scape by
bathing in water. — Although you bathe in water, lust will burn; and
though you climb the hill and hide you there, still lust will burn!
-----------

10. īki / Ch. 10. Liberality

91. koṭi mēlulakil iṉpamum koṭukkum.
illā iṭattum iyinta aḷaviṉāl
uḷḷa iṭampōl perituvantu — mellak
koṭiyoṭu paṭṭa kuṇaṉuṭi māntark
kaṭiyāvām āṇṭik katavu.
Unchilled by adversity the good do good : heaven's gate is open to them.
Even in their adverse hour, up to the measure of their means, as in the
prosperous times, with large rejoicing heart to give is their nature
still. To such good men heaven's gate is never closed.
92. mūppup piṇivarumuṉ pakuttuṇṇuka.
muṉṉarē cānāḷ muṉitakka mūppuḷa
piṉṉarum pīṭaḻikkum nōyuḷa — koṉṉē
paravaṉmiṉ paṟṟaṉmiṉ pāttuṇmiṉ yātuṅ
karavaṉmiṉ kittuṇṭām pōḻtu.
Death, old age, disease stand round. Give!
Before (you) are death's day and age detestable ; behind is disease that
humbles pride. Discursive thoughts indulge not. Cling not to earth. Eat,
sharing food; hide not your powers while wealth is in your hand.
93. celvattit tuyttalum koṭuttalum ciṟappām.
naṭukkuṟṟut taṟcērntār tuṉpantuṭiyār
koṭuttuttāṉ tuyppiṉum īṇṭuṅkāl īṇṭum
iṭukkuṟṟup paṟṟiṉum nillātu celvam
viṭukkum viṉiyulantak kāl.
It is useless saving: fate gives and takes away.
Though dread of want they do not relieve the woes of men who as
suppliants draw near! Yet although men enjoy and give, wealth grows in
growing time: cling to it, and yet it flies when former deeds that
brought wealth have lost their power.
94. iyalvatu koṭuttalē ēṟṟavāḻ vākum.
immi aricit tuṇiyāṉum vikalum
nummil iyiva koṭuttuṇmiṉ — ummik
koṭāa tavareṉpar kuṇṭunīr viyat
taṭāa aṭuppi ṉavar.
Give according to your power.
Daily having given somewhat, though but the fraction of a grin of rice,
according to your ability, eat ye (your own food).
Those who gave not in that world, men say on this earth girt by deep
waters are those on whose hearth nothing is cooked.
95. iravāmi koṭiyiṉum irumaṭaṅku nallatu.
maṟumiyum immiyum nōkki oruvark
kuṟumā ṟiyiva koṭuttal — vaṟumiyāl
ītal iciyā teṉiṉum iravāmi
ītal iraṭṭi yuṟum.
Do charity for the sake of this world and the future ; and beg not.
Regarding the other world and this world, give to any suppliant, in
fitting way, according to your ability. And if on account of poverty
giving is not possible, yet refrining from begging is twice as
meritorious as giving.
96. pakuttuṇṭu vāḻvatē payaṉuṭiya vāḻvu.
naṭuvūruḷ vētiki cuṟṟukkōṭ pukka
paṭupaṉi aṉṉar palarnacca vāḻvār
kuṭikoḻuttakkak kaṇṇuṅ koṭuttuṇṇāmākkaḷ
iṭukāṭṭuḷ ēṟṟip paṉi.
The fertile and sterile palms.
Those who live desired of many are as a fertile palm entered in the
altar's enclosure in midmost of the town. Those who, even when their
house grows great, give not before they eat, are like the sterile palm
in the burning ground.
97. uyarntōr utaviyāl ulakam nilipeṟum.
peyaṟpāl maḻipeyyāk kaṇṇum ulakam
ceyaṟpāla ceyyā viṭiṉum — kayaṟpulāl
puṉṉi kaṭiyum porukaṭal taṇcērppa
eṉṉi ulakuyyum āṟu.
In troublous time charity must not be omitted.
Even when the rin rins not in due season, if all the world should fail
in virtuous actions that ought to be done, —Lord of the warring sea's
cool shore, where acrid fume of fish in Punni's perfume dies! - how
scapes the world?
98. etir utavi vēṇṭi yītal koṭiyaṉṟu.
ēṟṟaki māṟṟāmi eṉṉāṉum tāmvariyār
āṟṟātārk kīvatām āṇkaṭaṉ — āṟṟiṉ
malikaṭal taṇcērppa māṟivārk kītal
polikaṭaṉ eṉṉum peyarttu.
Give to him who cannot recompense thee.
Denying to no out-stretched hand, to give to needy men as he hath power,
is duty of a man.—Lord of the swelling sea's cool shore !—A gift to
those that can return the gift is usury !
99. illi yeṉṉāmaṟ ciṟitēṉum īka.
iṟappac ciṟiteṉṉā tilleṉṉā teṉṟum
aṟappayaṉ yārmāṭṭuñ ceyka — muṟipputaviṉ
aiyam pukūun tavaci kaṭiñipōṟ
piya niṟittu viṭum.
Give though you have but little. The beggar's dish is filled in time.
Say not ‘ 'tis passing little,' nor ‘ ' tis nought I give;' on all
confer thy boon of virtuous charity, Like the dish the mendicant
presents from door to door, by frequent doles 'twill be filled full.
100. viyaka muḻuvatum vaḷḷalcīr paravum.
kaṭippiṭu kaṇmuracam kātattōr kēṭpar
iṭittu muḻaṅkiyatōr yōcaṉiyōr kēṭpar
aṭukkiya mūvulakum kēṭkumē cāṉṟōr
koṭuttā reṉappaṭum col.
The fame of charitable deeds.
The sound of beaten drum a katham off they'll hear; the thunder's voice
through a whole yojani will reach men's ears; the three successive
worlds will hear the word that says, ‘Men excellent their gifts have
given.'
------------

11. paḻaviṉi / Ch. 11. Old Deeds

101.muṉceyta viṉi ceytavaṉiyē tēṭivarum.
pallāvuḷ uyttu viṭiṉum kuḻakkaṉṟu
vallatām tāynāṭik kōṭalit — tollip
paḻaviṉiyum aṉṉa takittētaṟ ceyta
kiḻavaṉi nāṭik koḷaṟku.
Deeds come home to the doer.
Although you send forth the tender calf amid many cows, it has unerring
skill to seek out its own mother. Deeds of old days have even so the
power to search him out to whom their fruit pertins.
102.nalviṉi ceyyātavaṉ vāḻvu payaṉ aṟṟatu.
uruvum iḷamiyum oṇporuḷum uṭkum
oruvaḻi nillāmi kaṇṭum — oruvaḻi
oṉṟēyum illātāṉ vāḻkki uṭampiṭṭu
niṉṟuvīḻn takka tuṭittu.
A merely animal life.
Beauty and youth, and glittering wealth and reverence abide not in one
stay. To him who, though he sees this, does no single virtuous act in
this one stage of being, life's joy stands with the body and falls with
it.
103. celvatti yāvarum virumpuvar.
vaḷampaṭa vēṇṭātār yāryāru milli ;
aḷantaṉa pōkam avaravar āṟṟāl;
viḷaṅkāy tiraṭṭiṉār illi; kaḷaṅkaṉiyik
kāreṉac ceytārum il.
Wishes are inoperative.
Who would not see Prosperity? All seek her gifts; but as men's ways are,
so each man's enjoyments are meted out. Who made the Vilam's apple
round? Or who gave its dusky hue to the Kalam fruit ?
104. avaravarkku varum iṉpatuṉpaṅkaḷ akaṟṟamuṭiyā.
uṟaṟpāla nīkkal uṟuvarkkum ākā
peṟaṟpā laṉiyavum aṉṉavām; māri
vaṟappiṉ taruvārum illi; ataṉic
ciṟappiṉ taṇippārum il.
What must be, must be.
Not even sints can drive away predestined ills; and all the fated gin
must needs accrue. In time of drought who can bestow the rin ? Or who
can check its rich abundance when it falls?
105. paḻaviṉip payaṉāṟ periyōrum perumikeṭṭu vāḻvar.
tiṉittuṇiya rākittam tēcuuḷ aṭakkip
paṉittuṇiyār vikalum pāṭaḻintu vāḻvar
niṉippak kiṭanta tevaṉuṇṭām mēli
viṉippaya ṉallāṟ piṟa.
Vicissitudes of life are fate.
Those who lose like stately palms, when their greatness is gone, become
small asthe millet seed, hiding their glory within, —and so they pass
their days. This is the fruit of deeds of former days: when you think of
it, what other cuse can there be?
106.iḷamiyil aṟiñar iṟappatum viṉippayaṉ.
pallāṉṟa kēḷvip payaṉuṇarvār vīyavum
kallātār vāḻva taṟitirēl — kallātār
cētaṉa meṉṉumac cēṟkat tiṉmiyāl
kōteṉṟu koḷḷātām kūṟṟu.
Why ignorant men live, while the wise die.
Those that know the fruit of varied and profound learning die off, while
the unlearned joyously live on. Would you know the cuse ? — The
unlearned posses within no 'sap of sapience'; — so death deems then
refuse stalks, and takes them not!
107. picciyeṭuppatum muṟpiṟappiṟ ceyta viṉippayaṉtāṉ.
iṭumpikūr neñcattār ellāruṅ kāṇa
neṭuṅkaṭi niṉṟuḻalva tellām — aṭampampū
aṉṉam kiḻikkum alikaṭal taṇcērppa!
muṉṉi viṉiyāy viṭum.
Why some beg from door to door.
Lord of the sea's cool shore, where amid the wave swans sport, tearing
to shreds the Adambu flowers ! When those whose hearts are sore with
urgent need standbegging, and wander through the long street, in sight
of all, this is the fruit of former deeds.
108. ceyta viṉiyāl aṟiñarum tīviṉi purivar.
aṟiyārum allar aṟiva taṟintum
paḻiyōṭu paṭṭavi ceytal — vaḷiyōṭi
neytal naṟavuyirkkum nīḷkaṭal taṇcērppa!
ceyta viṉiyāṉ varum.
Why even wise men sin.
They are not ignorant; but, though what man should know they know, yet
they do deeds that bring guilt to their souls. — Lord of the wide sea's
pleasant shore, where breezes breathe the lily's fragrance round ! —
This comes from former deeds.
109.varuvaṉa vantucērum oruvaṉukku.
īṇṭunīr viyattuḷ ellārum ettuṇiyum
vēṇṭārmaṉ tīya ; viḻipayaṉ nallavi
vēṇṭiṉum vēṇṭāviṭiṉum uṟaṟpāla
tīṇṭā viṭutal aritu.
Desires are unaviling.
On the earth begirt by gathering waters no men desire in anywise evil
things, but choice fruit of good things. Yet whether they desire, or
abhor, it is hard to shun the touch of what fate assigns.
110.āvaṉa ākum; pōvaṉa pōkum: varuntuvatu payaṉiṉṟu.
ciṟukā perukā muṟipiṟaḻntu vārā
uṟukālat tūṟṟākā āmiṭattē ākum
ciṟukālip paṭṭa poṟiyum ataṉāl
iṟukālat teṉṉi parivu.
Fate is unalterable. Why grieve?
The early fates diminish not, nor do they increase, they come not in
order changedhelp in troublous times is none; what haps will happen,
there and then; and so, when all things fail, why grieve?
-----------

12. meymmi / Ch. 12. Truth: Reality

111.iyaṉṟati vaḻaṅkātiruppatu kuṟṟam.
iciyā oruporuḷ ileṉṟāl yārkkum
vaciyaṉṟu viyat tiyaṟki — naciaḻuṅka
niṉṟōṭip poyttal niritoṭīi! ceynnaṉṟi
koṉṟāriṉ kuṟṟa muṭittu.
Broken promises.
O mid with many armlets graced! To answer 'no' disgraces no man, when
the boon asked exceeds his means. It is the world's course. But to delay
and cheat the soul faint with desire is a sin like his who 'slays' a
benefit conferred.
112.evarum tattam iyaṟkiyiṉ niṟpar.
takkārum takkavar allārum tamnīrmi
ekkālum kuṉṟal ilarāvar —akkāram
yāvarē tiṉṉiṉum kiyātām kikkumām
tēvarē tiṉṉiṉum vēmpu.
Natures change not.
Men of worth, and men unworthy too, retin their natures ever unchanged.
Whoever they be that eat it, sugar can never grow bitter; and margosa is
bitter even when eaten by gods.
113.celvam vantapōtu ellārum cērvar.
kālāṭu pōḻtil kaḻikiḷiñar vāṉattu
mēlāṭu mīṉiṉ palarāvar — elā
iṭaroruvar uṟṟakkāl īrṅkuṉṟa nāṭa!
toṭarpuṭiyēm eṉpār cilar.
Friends in adverse and prosperous time.
When a man moves prosperonsly on, devoted kinsfolk are countless as
shining stars that move in the upper heaven. But when grievous
affliction haps,—Lord of the dripping hills !—few clim close alliance
with him.
114. celvattāl iṉpamum tarumamum eytalām.
vaṭuvilā viyattu maṉṉiya mūṉṟil
naṭuvaṇa teyta irutaliyum eytum!
naṭuvaṇa teytātāṉ eytum ulippey
taṭuvatu pōlum tuyar.
Wealth the essential things. Virtue, wealth, and pleasure.
Of the three things that endure upon this faultless earth, he who gins
the midmost gins the two extremes also. He who gins not the midmost
gins the cruel smart that the turtle feels when put into the pot to
boil.
115. celvar vāyccollē cellum eṅkum.
nalāviṉ kaṉṟāyiṉ nākum vilipeṟūum
kallārē āyiṉum celvarvāyccol cellum
pulīrap pōḻtiṉ uḻavēpōl mītāṭic
cellāvām nalkūrntār col.
The words of the rich and of the poor.
A young heifer fetches a good price when it is the calf of a good cow;
so the words of rich men though unlearned, pass current. Poor men's
words, like the plough when moisture is scanty, merely graze the surface
and are of no avil.
116. aṟivilār nūlkaṟṟālum aṭaṅkār.
iṭampaṭa meyññāṉam kaṟpiṉum eṉṟum
aṭaṅkātār eṉṟum aṭaṅkār — taṭaṅkaṇṇāy
uppoṭu neypāl tayirkāyam peytaṭiṉum
kippaṟā pēyccuriyiṉ kāy.
Wisdom cannot benefit the undisciplined.
O wide-eyed one! though you cook the wild gourd pouring in salt, ghee,
milk, curds, and spices, it never loses its bitterness. So those who
never discipline themselves, though they may learn extensive works of
true wisdom, never become disciplined.
117. matiyātavarit tāmum matiyātu viṭutalē māṇpu
tammi ikaḻvārit tāmavariṉ muṉikaḻka
eṉṉi avaroṭu paṭṭatu? —puṉṉi
viṟalpūṅ kamaḻkāṉal vīṅkunīrc cērppa!
uṟaṟpāla yārkkum uṟum.
Scorn the scorners.
When men scorn you, before their faces scorn them too ! what has a man
to do with them? — Lord of shore where beauteous Punni-flowers perfume
the glades that surround the swelling tide, — what fated comes to all!
118. aṟaṅkaḷ yāvum orutaṉmi yuṭiyaṉa.
āvē ṟuruviṉa āyiṉum āpayanta
pālvē ṟuruviṉa allavām — pāl pōl
orutaṉmit tākum aṟaneṟi āpōl
uruvu palakoḷal īṅku.
Cows of many colours, milk always white. Virtue one-many sects.
Though cows in form are diverse, the milk they yield is not diverse. The
way of virtue, like that milk, is one in nature, though the schools that
teach it here are like those cows, of many forms.
119. ellārum tuṉpamum iṉpamum aṭivar.
yāar ulakattōr col illār tēruṅkāl
yāar upāyattiṉ vāḻātār — yāar
iṭiyāka iṉṉāta teytātār yāar
kaṭipōkac celvam uyt tār
Four questions. The common lot.
Look well! of whom hath not the world found word to say ? And who have
not by prudence prospered in life's way? Ah! who in life's mid course no
bitter grief have known ? Ah! who to end of life have kept their wealth
their own?
120. uyiruṭaṉ celvatu ceyviṉi yoṉṟē.
tām ceyviṉiyallāl tammoṭu celvatumaṟ(ṟu)
yāṅkaṇum tēriṉ piṟitilli — āṅkuttām
pōṟṟip puṉinta uṭampum payamiṉṟē
kūṟṟamkoṇ ṭōṭum poḻutu.
Nothing accompanies in death but deeds.
Save a man's deed: nought goes with him, search where you will. The body
which men cherish so, and adorn, is itself profitless indeed when death
shall seize and hurry off with it.
------

13. tīviṉiyaccam / Ch. 13. Dread of Evil Deeds

121. piṟavuyirkaḷik koṉṟu pulāluṇṇal perumpāvam.
tukkattuḷ tūṅkit tuṟaviṉkaṇ cērkalā
makkaṭ piṇatta cuṭukāṭu — tokka
vilaṅkiṟkum puḷḷiṟkum kāṭē pulaṉkeṭṭa
pullaṟi vāḷar vayiṟu.
Men lead lives of self-indulgence.
The burning ground is filled with the corpses of men that will not give
themselves up to a self renouncing life, but oscillate amid sorrows; and
the maws of perverted foolish men are a mere burning-ground for beasts
and birds.
122. paṟavikaḷik kūṭṭil aṭippatu pāvam.
irumpārkkuṅ kālarāy ētilārk kāḷāyk
karumpār kaḻaṉiyuṭ cērvar — curumpārkkum
kāṭṭuḷāy vāḻum civalum kuṟumpūḻum
kūṭṭuḷāyk koṇṭuvip pār.
Penalty for imprisoning birds.
Their legs in iron bound, as slaves to alien lords they will till the
black and barren soil, who snared and kept in cages partridges and
quails, that dwell in wilds where beetles hum amid the flowers.
123. paḻaviṉiyāl nāṉ toḻunōyum varum
akkēpōl aṅki oḻiya, viralaḻukit
tukkat toḻunōy eḻupavē — akkāl
alavaṉik kātalittuk kālmuṟittut tiṉṟa
paḻaviṉi vantaṭintak kāl.
The penalty incurred by crab-eaters.
Like fire their palms shall glow, their fingers rotaway, who loved in
other times on crabs to feed, and broke their joints, what time the
guilt of ‘olden deeds' comes home, and leprosy's fierce pangs assil.
124. tīyavarōṭu kūṭiṉ nalla varum tīyavarāvar
neruppaḻal cērntakkāl neypōl vatūum
erippaccaṭ ṭevvanōy ākkum — parappak
koṭuviṉiya rākuvar kōṭārum kōṭik
kaṭuviṉiyar ākiyārc cārntu.
Bad companions.
Even things (soft and soothing) like ghee, when joined with the fierce
heat of fire, will blaze and burn, and cuse bitter anguish : so even
upright men are perverted and give themselves up to deeds of utter evil,
when they attach themselves to those whose deeds are evil.
125. periyavar naṭpup piṟipōl vaḷarum
periyavar kēṇmi piṟipōla nāḷum
varici variciyā nantum — variciyāl
vāṉūr matiyam pōl vikalum tēyumē
tāṉē ciṟiyār toṭarpu.
Friendships with great and mean.
Great men's intimate regard will dily grow in order due like the
crescent moon. Mean men's alliance like the full moon that rides the sky
dily by degrees dwindles away of itself.
126. naṉṟāka āyntu naṭpukkoḷḷa vēṇṭum.
cāṉṟōreṉa matittuc cārntāymaṉ cārntāykkuc
cāṉṟāṇmi cārntārkaṇ illāyiṉ cārntōykēḷ
cāntakat tuṇṭeṉṟu cepput tiṟantoruvaṉ
pāmpakattuk kaṇṭa tuṭittu.
Disappointment and danger from foolish attachments.
Thou didst attach thyself (to unworthy persons), saying, they are men of
absolute integrity! If to thee who hast thus attached thyself, integrity
in those thine intimates does not appear; hear, O thou who hast so
attached thyself, it is as if one opened a casket, thinking it contined
an odorous unguent, and saw a snake within.
127. makkaḷ maṉamum ceyalum māṟupaṭṭiruppar
yāar oruvar oruvartam uḷḷattit
tērum tuṇimi uṭiyavar — cāral
kaṉamaṇi niṉṟimikkum nāṭa!kēḷ; makkaḷ
maṉam vēṟu ceykiyum vēṟu.
Man cannot fathom other men's minds.
What single man has power to search and clearly know the inmost self of
other men? Lord of the land where weighty gems glisten on mountin
slope, O hear ! — Men's minds are otherwise, and otherwise their deeds.
128. kaṉḷanaṭpuk karuttiṟku varuttam tarum
uḷḷattāṉ naḷḷā tuṟutit toḻilarāyk
kaḷḷattāṉ naṭṭār kaḻikēṇmi — teḷḷip
puṉaṟcetumpu niṉṟalikkum pūṅkuṉṟa nāṭa!
maṉattukkaṇ mācāy viṭum.
Friends from self-interest
Lord of the flowery hilly land where streams wash out and carry hither
and thither (precious things) from the marshy land ! — The effusive
friendship of those who do not attach themselves (to us) in heart, but
perform certin friendly acts merely to strengthen their own position,
and who form friendships guilefully, will issue in disappointment to the
minds (of those who are intimate with them).
129. aṟivillār naṭpiṉi añciviṭuttal nallatu
ōkkiya oḷvāḷtaṉ oṉṉārkippaṭṭakkāl
ūkkam aḻippatūum meyyākum — ākkam
irumiyum ceṉṟu cuṭutalāl nalla
karumamē kallārkaṇ tīrvu.
Ruin from unfitting intimacies.
If the glittering swords a man brandishes (be allowed to) fall into the
hands of his foes, it will assuredly come about that he will thus
destroy also his own power of action. So wealth (bestowed on the
foolish) will go and burn up (merit accruing in) both worlds, and
therefore the really good thing is to keep clear of the foolish.
130. nalviṉiyē uyirōṭu toṭarntu ceṉṟu naṉmi payakkum
maṉippācam kiviṭāy makkaṭkeṉ ṟēṅki
eṉittūḻi vāḻtiyō neñcē — eṉittum
ciṟuvariyē āyiṉum ceytanaṉ ṟallāl
uṟupayaṉō illi uyirkku.
To do good is life's gin.
O mind! thou leavest not the bonds of home. How many cycles, pray, with
thou live yearning still for children? Save the good he has done,
however small its measure may be, there is no true result to any living
soul.
aṟattuppāl muṟṟum. End of Part I.
-----------

pakuti 2. poruṭpāl / PART II. ON WEALTH 14. kalvi /Ch. 14. Learning

131. kalvi yaḻakē ciṟanta aḻaku.
kuñci yaḻakum koṭuntāṉik kōṭṭaḻakum
mañcaḷ aḻakum aḻakalla - neñcattu
nallamyām eṉṉum naṭuvu nilimiyāl
kalvi yaḻakē yaḻaku.
Learning, the only beauty.
Beauty of locks, beauty of circling garments' folds, beauty of saffron
tint: these are not beauty true. Integrity of soul that brings the
conscience peace is learning's gift: that only is beauty true!
132. aṟiyāmi nōyi yakaṟṟum maruntu kalvi
immi payakkumāl īyak kuṟiviṉṟāl
tammi viḷakkumāl tāmuḷarāk kēṭiṉṟāl
emmi yulakattum yāmkāṇēm kalvipōl
mammar aṟukkum maruntu
The remedy for bewilderment
Since in this world it yields fruit; since given it grows not less ;
since it makes men illustrious; since it perishes not as long as (its
possessors) themselves exist; in any world we see not any medicine that,
like learning, removes the delusions of sense.
133. kalipala kaṟṟavar talivar āvār.
kaḷarnilat tuppiṟanta uppiṉic cāṉṟōr
viḷinilattu nelliṉ viḻumitāk koḷvar;
kaṭinilattō rāyiṉum kaṟṟaṟin tōrit
talinilattu vikkap paṭum.
The learned, though low-born, are first.
The excellent regard the salt produced in brackish ground as choicer
than the Nel from fertile soil. It is fitting to place in the first rank
the learned wise, though (sprung) from the lowest origin.
134. makkaṭkuk kalviyē nili peṟṟa celvam.
vippuḻik kōṭpāṭā vāyttīyiṟ kēṭilli
mikka ciṟappiṉ aracar ceṟiṉvavvār
eccam eṉoruvaṉ makkaṭkuc ceyvaṉa
viccimaṟ ṟalla piṟa.
Learning, the best legacy.
It cannot be taken from its place of deposit; it does not perish
anywhere by fire; if kings of surpassing grandeur are angry they cannot
take it away; (and therefore) what any man should provide for his
children as a legacy is learning. Other things are not (real wealth).
135. uṇmi nūlkaḷiyē yuṇarntu kaṟkavēṇṭum.
kalvi kariyila kaṟpavar nāḷcila
mella niṉikkiṉ piṇipala — teḷḷitiṉ
ārāyn tamivuṭiya kaṟpavē nīroḻiyap
pāluṇ kurukiṉ terintu.
Discriminating study.
Learning hath no bounds, the learners' days are few. If you think calmly
diseases many wait around! With clear discrimination learn what is meet
for you, like the swan that leaving the water drinks the milk.
136. tāḻkulam uyarkulam pārātu kaṟṟavaṉi matikkavēṇṭum.
tōṇi iyakkuvāṉ tolli varuṇattuk
kāṇiṉ kaṭippaṭṭāṉ eṉṟikaḻār — kāṇāy
avaṉtuṇiyā āṟupōy aṟṟēnūl kaṟṟa
makaṉ tuṇiyā nalla koḷal.
Never mind the boatman's caste if he take you over!
As none contemn the ferryman, by old caste rule to lowest rank assigned,
but cross the stream by help he lends; so take thou teachings good and
wise by help of him who is the learned man.
137. kaṟṟōruṭaṉ kūṭi uriyāṭi makiḻvatē pēriṉpam ākum.
tavalarun tolkēḷvit taṉmi yuṭiyār
ikalilar eḵkuṭiyār tammuḷ kuḻīi
nakaliṉ iṉitāyiṉ kāṇpām akalvāṉat
tumpar uṟivār pati.
The supreme enjoyment of the society of learned and amiable men.
We shall see whether any greater bliss is found in the city inhabited by
the dwellers in the ample heaven than is felt when men of natures formed
by old imperishable lore, from rivalries exempt keen as tempered steel,
meet together and lugh.
138. kaṟṟavar naṭpu vaḷaravaḷara iṉpam tarum.
kaṉikaṭal taṇcērppa! kaṟṟaṟintār kēṇmi
nuṉiyiṉ karumputiṉṟaṟṟē — nuṉinīkkit
tūriṉtiṉ ṟaṉṉa takittarō paṇpilā
īra milāḷar toṭarpu.
The friendship of the learned ever grows sweeter, that of the unlearned
ever
diminishes in sweetness.
Lord of the cool shore of the resounding sea intimacy with learned
people is like eating sugar cane from the (tender, juicy) tip;
association with graceless. sapless men is like leaving the (tender) tip
and eating it from the (hard, dry) root.
139. kaṟṟavaric cārntāṟ kallārum nallaṟivu peṟuvar.
kallārē yāyiṉum kaṟṟāric cērntoḻukiṉ
nallaṟivu nāḷum talippaṭuvar — tolciṟappiṉ
oṇṇiṟap pātirippūc cērtalāl puttōṭu
taṇṇīrkkut tāṉpayan tāṅku.
