The benefits of association with the learned. The pot impregnated with
odour. Though themselves unlearned, if men live in association with the
learned they advance dily in excellent knowledge. The new vessel, by
contact with the Padri-flower of old renown and lustrous hue, imparts
fragrance to the cold water it contins.
140. aṟivu nūl kaṟṟāl mayakkam nīkkum.
alakucāl kaṟpiṉ aṟivunūl kallā
tulakanūl ōtuva tellām — kalakala
kūum tuṇiyallāl koṇṭu taṭumāṟṟam
pōom tuṇiyaṟivār il.
Books of wisdom are the best. Others cannot remove confusion of mind.
If men leaving works of wisdom, that contin well-weighed instruction,
unstudied, devote themselves to the recitation of mere worldly
literature, they will acquire a store of empty high-sounding words, but
not that wisdom by means of which mental confusion (that treats unreal
things as real) is removed.
------------

15. kuṭippiṟappu / Ch. 15. High Birth

141. uyarkuṭippiṟantār vaṟumiyilum oḻukkam tavaṟār.
uṭukki ulaṟi uṭampaḻintak kaṇṇum
kuṭippiṟap pāḷartam koḷkiyiṟ kuṉṟār
iṭukkaṇ talivantak kaṇṇum arimā
koṭippul kaṟikkumō maṟṟu.
In adversity noble men do not desert their principles.
Though their clothes may be old and their body worn with want, men of
noble birth diminish nothing of their due observances. Will the lion
nibble the creeping grass although sorest need should assil him?
142. kīḻmakkaḷ celvam vantālum peruntakimi yaṭiyār.
cāṉṟāṇmi cāyal oḻukkam ivimūṉṟum
vāṉtōy kuṭippiṟantārk kallatu — vāṉtōyum
mitavaḻ veṟpa! paṭāa peruñcelvam
eytiyak kaṇṇum piṟarkku.
The high-born only have perfect excellence, greatness, and good manners.
Lord of the hills traversed by clouds that touch the heavens! true
excellence, and dignity, and good conduct, — these three things belong
to men of race that touches heaven, and not to others, even though they
may have acquired great wealth.
143. kuṭippiṟantavarkkē aṭakkam uriyatu.
irukki eḻalum etircelavum ēṉi
viṭuppa oḻitalō ṭiṉṉa — kuṭippiṟantār
kuṉṟā oḻukkamāk koṇṭār ; kayavarō
ṭoṉṟā uṇaraṟpāṟ ṟaṉṟu.
The noble only have an instinctive sense of propriety. Rising from their
seat (at the approach of worshipful persons), going forth to meet them,
departing when they dismiss, and such - like things, the well born
mintin as invariable decorum. The low understand not one of these
things.
144. naṟkuṭip piṟappiṉāl naṟpaṇpu cērum.
nallavi ceyyiṉ iyalpākum tīyavi
pallavar tūṟṟum paḻiyākum — ellām
uṇaruṅ kuṭippiṟappiṉ matiyam eṉṉō
puṇarum oruvark keṉiṉ.
Noble birth makes duty easy.
If men (of noble birth) do good things it is natural to them. As to evil
things (to commit these is impossible to them); for this would be guilt
which many would bruit abroad. What greater good then can accrue to men
than high-birth, if it be their lot, to which the perception of all
(these things) belongs!
145. tīcceyalkaḷ ceyvataṟku añcuvar naṟkuṭiyiṟ piṟantōr.
kallāmi accam kayavar toḻilaccam
collāmi yuḷḷumōr cōrvaccam — ellām
irappārkkoṉ ṟīyāmi accam marattārim
māṇāk kuṭippiṟan tār.
These four pious fears exist only among the really worthy.
Dread of unlearned ignorance; dread of the work that base men do; dread
of forgetful slip in words which one must not utter; dread of not giving
to those that ask: those who are born of a race not so distinguished by
conscientious fears are as trees.
146. iṉiyacol , īki mutaliya naṟkuṇaṅkaḷ kuṭippiṟantōr iṭattirukkum.
iṉanaṉmi iṉcoloṉ ṟītalmaṟ ṟēṉi
maṉa naṉmi eṉaṟivi yellām — kaṉamaṇi
mūttō ṭimikkum muḻaṅkuvarit taṇcērppa
iṟpiṟantār kaṇṇē yuḷa.
The fine qualities of the high-born.
Lord of the roaring salt-sea's cool shore, where gleam rare gems with
pearls!Association with the good, pleasant speech, a liberal hand, and
purity of mind, —all these are only found among the nobly born.
147. kuṭippiṟantavar tuṉpam vantālum naṟceyalē purivar
ceyki yaḻintu citalmaṇṭiṟ ṟāyiṉum
peyyā oruciṟi pēril uṭittākum;
evvam uḻantak kaṭittum kuṭippiṟantār
ceyvar ceyaṟpā lavi.
High-born men do their duty always.
Though its frame-work has perished, and thronging white ants infest it,
in a spacious mansion some room will still afford shelter from the rin.
Thus, although want annoys them, the nobly born even yet will do what
should be done.
148. kuṭippiṟantār vaṟumiyilum piṟarkkutavi purivār.
orupuṭi pāmpu koḷiṉum orupuṭi
aṅkaṇmā ñālam viḷakkuṟūum — tiṅkaḷpōl
cellāmi cevvaṉnēr niṟpiṉum oppuraviṟ
kolkār kuṭippiṟan tār.
The moon when half in the serpent's mouth still gives light.
Like the moon which affords light to the fair and spacious earth with
one side, while the dragon holds the other, the nobly born do not become
remiss in works of seemly benevolence, though poverty (inability) stand
fronting them.
149. kīḻmakkaḷ celvattilum utavi ceyyār.
cellā iṭattum kuṭippiṟantār ceyvaṉa
celliṭattum ceyyār ciṟiyavar — pulvāy
parumam poṟuppiṉum pāypari māpōl
porumuraṇ āṟṟutal iṉṟu.
The deer becomes not a charger.
Men of mean descent, even when all goes well with them, will not perform
the good deeds that the well-born will do, even when things do not go
well with them.
Though the antelope should bear a pillion, it rushes not to war like the
prancing charger.
150. tammiyaṭintavarit tāṅkuvar kuṭippiṟantōr.
eṟṟoṉṟum illā viṭattum kuṭippiṟantār
aṟṟuttaṟ cērntārk kaciviṭattu ūṟṟāvar
aṟṟak kaṭittum akalyā ṟakaḻntakkāl
teṟṟeṉat teṇṇīr paṭum.
The noble even in poverty afford aid do those who seek it.
The nobly born, even when destitute, are props in time of feebleness to
the needy ones that draw nigh to them. So, when the wide river is dry,
if you dig in its bed, forth with clear waters gush out.
-------------

16. mēṉmakkaḷ /Ch. 16. Great Men

151. ciṟu kuṟṟamum mēṉmakkaḷiṭam cērātu.
aṅkaṇ vicumpiṉ acalnilāp pārikkun
tiṅkaḷum cāṉṟōrum opparmaṉ — tiṅkaḷ
maṟuvāṟṟum cāṉṟōr aḵ tāṟṟār terumantu
tēyvar orumā cuṟiṉ.
The good cannot endure a stin.
The moon that diffuses light through heaven's fair realms, and truly
worthy men are alike: yet that endures a spot, while the truly worthy
endure it. not; — perplexed and sad they pine away if but one stin
appear.
152. arumiyāṉa ceyalkaḷiyē mēṉmakkaḷ ceyvar.
iciyum eṉiṉum iciyā teṉiṉum
vacitīra eṇṇuvar cāṉṟōr — viciyiṉ
narimā vuḷaṅkiḻitta ampiṉil tītō
arimāp piḻippeyta kōl.
Aim high! Better miss a lion, than hit a jackal
Whether success attend, or do not attend the work, the excellent will
even ponder blameless ends.—Is the shaft that missed the lion worse than
the arrow sent forth, that with its impulse pierced the jackal's heart?
153. mēlōr vaṟumiyilum ceyyattakkavaṟṟiyē ceyvār.
narampeḻuntu nalkūrntā rāyiṉum cāṉṟōr
kurampeḻuntu kuṟṟaṅkoṇ ṭēṟār — uramkavaṟā
uḷḷameṉum nāriṉāṟ kaṭṭi uḷavariyāl
ceyvar ceyaṟpā lavi.
The excellent are not led by want to commit evil.
The excellent though emaciated and poor, do not transgress the limits of
virtue and commit evil. With wisdom for the pillar, with perseverance as
the band, they bind (the mind); and as long as they live they do what it
behoves them to do.
154. mēlōri oru nāṭ kaṇṭālum paḻami naṇparāvar.
celvuḻik kaṇorunāḷ kāṇiṉum cāṉṟavar
tolvaḻik kēṇmiyiṉ tōṉṟap purintiyāppar
nalvari nāṭa! cilanāḷ aṭippaṭiṉ
kalvariyum uṇṭām neṟi.
The good never forget even a casual acquaintance.
Lord of the land of goodly hills ! —If trodden for a few days a path is
formed over even the craggy hill; so excellent persons, though they have
seen (worthy) men only for one day, as they were travelling, will bind
them to their soul, exhibiting all the marks of an ancient friendship.
155. kallār uriyiyum nallōr kēṭpār.
pullā eḻuttiṉ poruḷil vaṟuṅkōṭṭi
kallā oruvaṉ urippavum, kaṇṇōṭi
nallār varuntiyuṅ kēṭparē; maṟṟavaṉ
pallāruḷ nāṇal parintu.
The good listen with patient courtesy to the orations of the ignorant.
Even when one speaks who has an ungrammatical knowledge of the letter,
but not of the meaning who is of a low (empty) school, and is unlearned
the good with kindly compassion will listen, though it is pin to them,
being grieved that he should be put to shame in the presence of many.
156. vitālum cāṉṟōr vaṭuvuri kūṟār.
kaṭittuk karumpiṉik kaṇtakara nūṟi
iṭittunīr koḷḷiṉum iṉcuvittē ākum
vaṭuppaṭa vituiṟantak kaṇṇum kuṭippiṟantār
kūṟār tam vāyiṟ citintu.
Sugar-cane, crush it as you will, is ever sweet.
Although you bite the sugar-cane, crush it till its joints are broken,
grind it, and express its juice, it still will be sweet. The highly
born, even when men have passed by abusing them so as to wound, never
lose their self-respect so as to utter from their mouth (words of
abuse).
157. mēlōr poy mutaliya pāvaṅkaḷil etuvum ceyyār.
kaḷḷārkaḷ uṇṇār kaṭiva kaṭintorīi
eḷḷip piṟari ikaḻnturiyār — taḷḷiyum
vāyiṉpoy kūṟār vaṭuvaṟu kāṭciyār
cāyiṉ pariva tilar.
The wise free from gross evils.
They defrud not, drink not palm-wine, shun what is forbidden, never
despise and speak contemptuously of their neighbours, nor even
forgetfully do they utter anything untrue with their mouth : these men
of faultless vision grieve not though they fall into distress.
158. piṟarmaṟi kēḷāmi, piṟarmaṉi viḻiyāmi , puṟaṅkūṟāmi immūṉṟum ciṟanta
nalloḻukkaṅkaḷ.
piṟarmaṟi yiṉkaṇ ceviṭāyt tīya
ētilār ilkaṇ kuruṭaṉāyt tiṟaṉaṟintu
puṟaṅkūṟṟiṉ mūkiyāy niṟpāṉēl yātum
aṟaṅkūṟa vēṇṭā avaṟku.
When a man should be deaf, blind, and dumb.
Deaf to others' secrets, blind to his neighbour's wife, dumb to evil
backbiting—if, knowing what is befitting, a man thus abides, it is not
necessary to teach him any virtuous precepts.
159. mēṉ makkaḷiṭattiṟ pala nāṭ ceṉṟālum mutaṉāṭ kaṇṭatupōlavē matippār.
paṉṉāḷum ceṉṟakkāl paṇpilār tam uḻi
eṉṉāṉum vēṇṭupa eṉṟikaḻpa — eṉṉāṉum
vēṇṭiṉum naṉṟumaṟ ṟeṉṟu viḻumiyōr
kāṇṭoṟum ceyvar ciṟappu.
Seek the good they are always kind.
They who are destitute of kindly courtesy, even after the lapse of many
days, will contemn (those that visit them), saying, ‘they will ask
something of us'. The excellent will treat them with distinction
whenever they see them, saying, ‘if they ask something of us, it is
well'.
160. mēṉmakkaḷic cārntavar mēṉmi aṭivār.
uṭiyār ivareṉ ṟorutaliyāp paṟṟik
kaṭiyāyar piṉceṉṟu vāḻvar — uṭiya
pilamtalip paṭṭatu pōlātē, nalla
kulamtalip paṭṭa iṭattu.
The treasure cave.
The lowest sort of men say, there are men of property,’— cling to them
steadily, follow them, and so flourish. Is it not as when a mine of
treasure has been found, when men of good lineage have become our
friends.
--------------

17. periyārip piḻiyāmi / Ch. 17. Avoidance of Offence to the Great

161. periyavar veṟuttāl piṉ tiruppa (pērkka) muṭiyātu.
poṟuppareṉ ṟeṇṇip puritīrntār māṭṭum
veṟuppaṉa ceyyāmi vēṇṭum — veṟuttapiṉ
ārkkum aruvi aṇimali nalnāṭa!
pērkkutal yārkkum aritu.
It is difficult to regin the forfeited favour of good men.
Lord of the pleasant land whose hills resounding water-falls adorn ! —
You may not even to faultless men do things that enkindle wrath; for
when their wrath is once kindled, it is hard for any one to change their
mood.
162. aṟivillātavar periyavari aṭuttiruntālum payaṉpeṟār.
poṉṉē koṭuttum puṇartaṟ kariyārik
koṉṉē talikkūṭap peṟṟiruntum — aṉṉō
payaṉil poḻutāk kaḻipparē nalla
nayamil aṟivi ṉavar.
Waste of golden opportunities.
Although those whose (pretended) wisdom is without moral excellence have
gined access to men to whom approach is difficult even by gifts of gold
alas! they pass away (the precious moments) as mere waste time.
163. periyavar matippatuvē matippākum.
avamatippum āṉṟa matippum iraṇṭum
mikimakka ḷāṉmatikkaṟ pāla — nayamuṇarāk
kiyaṟiyā mākkaḷ iḻippum eṭuttēttum
viyār vaṭitta nūlār.
Prise and disprise of noble persons only of importance.
Both disesteem and thorough esteem are in the class of things that
depend on the estimation of the great. Those who have a discriminating
knowledge of true science, lay no stress on either the abuse or the
fulsome prise of man who comprehend not moral principles, and know not
the rules of propriety.
164. periyavar ciṉantāṟ piḻippatu aritu.
virinila nākam viṭaruḷa tēṉum
urumiṉ kaṭuñciṉam cēṇiṉṟum uṭkum
arumi uṭiya araṇcērntum uyyār
perumi uṭiyār ceṟiṉ.
None safe from the wrath of the great.
The serpent rich of hue, though it dwell in the mountin-cleft, from far
is frighted by the thunderbolt's fierce wrath; so men escape not though
hid in strongholds hard to reach, when those great in virtue are wroth.
165. tammit tāmē matittukkoḷvatu matippākātu
emmi aṟintiliṉ empōlvar illeṉṟu
tammittām koḷvatu kōḷaṉṟu — tammi
ariyarā nōkki araṉaṟiyuñ cāṉṟōr
periyarāk koḷvatu kōḷ.
Self-prise is no commendation.
‘You know not all our worth, for equals have we none:’ when self thus
estimates itself, this is not ‘esteem’ ! When perfect men, proficients
in virtue, regard any as dear, and esteem them as great, this is
‘esteem’ !
166. periyavar kēṇmi nāḷukku nāḷ perukum.
naḷikaṭal taṇcērppa! nāḷniḻal pōla
viḷiyum ciṟiyavar kēṇmi — viḷiviṉṟi
alku niḻalpōl akaṉṟakaṉ ṟōṭumē
tolpuka ḻāḷar toṭarpu.
Morning and evening shadows.
Lord of the great sea’scool margin !—Friendships formed with mean men
dwindling die like shadows of the morn; friendship of men of old renown
as shadows of the after-day will lengthen out and grow.
167. maṉṉar celvamum maṅkiyar iṉpamum aṭuttavar aṭivār.
maṉṉar tiruvum makaḷir eḻilnalamum
tuṉṉiyār tuyppar takalvēṇṭā — tuṉṉik
kuḻikoṇṭu tāḻnta kuḷirmara mellām
uḻitaṅkaṭ ceṉṟārk koruṅku.
To those who cultivate them fruits accrue.
Those who press their suit, fitness a part, enjoy the wealth of kings,
and the charms of midens fair; so all the cool (shady) trees that droop
earthward with thick foliage, give room to those that thronging seek
their shelter.
168. yāriṭattum naṭpuk koḷḷāmi naṉṟu.
teriyat teriyun terivilār kaṇṇum
piriyap perumpaṭarnōy ceyyum — periya
ulavā iruṅkaḻic cērppa!yār māṭṭuṅ
kalavāmi kōṭi uṟum.
Friendship brings pangs of severance.
Severance from even those who have no under standing [lit. no
understanding that understands (so as) to understand] cuses great and
spreading sorrow. — Lord of the shore of the great, unfailing, swelling
bay !—To be intimate with none is ten million times the best.
169. naṟceyal purinta nāṉē payaṉpaṭṭa nāṉ.
kallātu pōkiya nāḷum periyavarkaṭ
cellātu vikiya vikalum — olva
koṭāa toḻinta pakalum urippiṟ
paṭāavām paṇpuṭiyār kaṇ.
No day unimproved.
Days gone by without learning, those passed without any intercourse with
the great, those spent without giving what is fitting;— in the case of
the excellent, if you tell them over, none such occur.
170. periyōrkkum paṇivu, aṟiñarkku aṭakkam, celvarkkuk koṭi ciṟappit tarum.
periyār perumi ciṟutakimi oṉṟiṟ
kuriyār urimi aṭakkam — teriyuṅkāl
celva muṭiyārum celvarē taṟcērntār
allal kaḷipa veṉiṉ.
Humility. Self-restrint. Charity.
The greatness of the great is [the quality of littleness (in their own
sight), i. e.] humility; the (real) acquisition of those who have
acquired (any). one (science) is modest self-restrint. If you rightly
understand things, those possessors of wealth only are really wealthy
who relieve the wants of those that approach them (as suppliants).
----------------

18. nalliṉañcērtal /Ch. 18. Association with the Good

171. nallōric cārntāl tīyakuṇam nīṅkum.
aṟiyāp paruvat taṭaṅkārō ṭoṉṟi
neṟiyalla ceytoḻuki yavvum - neṟiyaṟinta
naṟcārvu cārak keṭumē veyilmuṟukap
puṟpaṉip paṟṟuviṭ ṭāṅku.
Good companionship cures the follies of youth.
In youth unwise, though men consort with hughty ones and walk in
lawless ways; yet, when they join with those that know the righteous
path, their faults shall melt away as dew from off the grass when
sunbeams scorch.
172. periyār vāyccoṟkēṭṭu aṟaneṟiyaṟintu vāḻka.
aṟimiṉ aṟaneṟi añcumiṉ kūṟṟam
poṟumiṉ piṟarkaṭuñcol pōṟṟumiṉ vañcam
veṟumiṉ viṉitīyār kēṇmiyeñ ñāṉṟum
peṟumiṉ periyārvāyc col.
Six precepts.
Know virtue's path ! Dread death! Beat others' words severe! Beware ye
practise no deceit! Friendship detest with men of evil deeds! Daily get
gin of words that fall from greatmen's lips!
173. piṟavit tuṉpatti veṟutta periyavar uṟavukoḷka.
aṭintārp pirivum arumpiṇiyum kēṭum
uṭaṅkuṭampu koṇṭārk kuṟalāl — toṭaṅkip
piṟappiṉṉā teṉṟuṇarum pēraṟivi ṉāri
uṟappuṇarka ammāeṉ neñcu .
Life has many evils. Attach yourself to the wise.
Severance of close-joined friends and sore disease and death, all these
combined hap to those that have assumed a human body: so the truly wise
have felt that birth, from the very first, is bitterness. Ah! soul,
cleave close to these!
174. nallōr naṭpu iruntāl piṟappil veṟuppuk koḷḷār.
iṟappa niṉiyuṅkāl iṉṉā teṉiṉum
piṟappiṉi yārum muṉiyār — piṟappiṉum
paṇpāṟṟu neñcat tavarkaḷō ṭeññāṉṟum
naṇpāṟṟi naṭkap peṟiṉ.
Friendship makes life endurable.
Though when you ponder it, it is surpassing: bitterness, none hate (this
mortal) birth, if in (this mortal) birth they ever perform friendly acts
to men whose hearts are set on noble excellence, and gin their
friendship.
175. māṭciyuṭiyāric cērntōr malipōl niṟpar.
ūraṅ kaṇanīr uravunīr cērntakkāl
pērum piṟitākit tīrttamām — ōruṅ
kulamāṭci yillārum kuṉṟupōl niṟpar
nalamāṭci nallāric cārntu.
The water from the sewer becomes a sacred stream.
When water from the town-sewers has joined the great river its very name
is changed, and it becomes a 'sacred stream’. Ponder well! Even those
who have no greatness of clan, if they ally themselves with good men of
goodly fame, shall stand firm as a hill.
176. periyavaric cērntōr perumi yaṭivār.
oṇkatir vāḷmatiyam cērtalāl ōṅkiya
aṅkaṇ vicumpiṉ muyalum toḻappaṭūum
kuṉṟiya cīrmiyarāyiṉum cīrpeṟuvar
kuṉṟaṉṉār kēṇmi koḷiṉ.
The hare in the moon is worshipped.
Becuse it is in the bright-beamed moon the very hare in heaven's lofty
fair expanse is worshipped. Though scant their worthiness, men obtin
worth who share friendship with those firm as a hill.
177. perumiyuṭiyavaric cārntāṟ ciṟumi nīṅkum.
pālō ṭaḷāyanīr pālākum allatu
nīrāy niṟanterintu tōṉṟātām — tēriṉ
ciṟiyār ciṟumiyum tōṉṟātām nalla
periyār perumiyic cārntu.
Water with milk seems milk.
Commingled with milk water becomes milk; at least, it shows no more as
water by its hue. Look close, the mean men's meanness shows not if they
join themselves to greatness of the good and great.
178. āṟṟaluṭiyavaric cārntōr yāvarkkum añcār.
kolli yirumpuṉattuk kuṟṟi yaṭintapul
olkāvē yākum uḻavar uḻupaṭikku
melliyarē yāyiṉum naṟcārvu cārntārmēl
cellāvām ceṟṟār ciṉam.
The society of the good a protection.
Grass close around the stump in the field adjoining the house will not
be destroyed by the ploughshare of the farmer ; [thus] though men are
weak, if they get under safe protection, the wrath of their foes will
not reach them.
179. tīyavar iṉam cērntāl naṟpaṇpu nīṅkum.
nilanalattāl nantiya nellēpōl tattam
kulanalattāl ākuvar cāṉṟōr — kalanalattit
tīvaḷi ceṉṟu citittāṅkuc cāṉṟāṇmi
tīyiṉam cērak keṭum.
Good tribal associations mintin excellence, and evil ones wreck it.
As Nel that flourishes through the goodness of the soil, good men become
illustrious by the excellence of their respective clanships.
Goodness perishes when it comes near evil associates, as the tornado
advances and destroys the excellence of the ship.
180. tīyavarōṭu cērntavarum keṭuvār.
maṉattāṉ maṟuvila rēṉuntāñ cērnta
iṉattāl ikaḻap paṭuvar — puṉattu
veṟikamaḻ cantaṉamum vēṅkiyum vēmē
eṟipuṉan tīppaṭṭak kāl.
Evil association brings ruin.
Though themselves pure in mind, even good men incur contempt becuse of
their associations. So in the woodland glade when a conflagration takes
place (not the worthless brushWood only, but) the fragrant Sandal and
Vengi too are consumed.
--------------

19. perumi / Ch. 19. (Moral) Greatness

181. pērāci nīkkiyavarē perumi yuṭiyavar.
īta liciyā tiḷamicēṇ nīṅkutalāl
kāta lavaruṅ karuttallar — kātalit
tātumnām eṉṉum avāviṉik kiviṭṭup
pōvatē pōlum poruḷ.
Renunciation of desire is ‘greatness.'
To give is no longer ours; and youth is gone far off ; our once beloved
ones think of us no more! To depart, having abandoned 'desire' that bids
us love and hope for future joys, appears the thing that's meet.
182. vāḻvu niliyeṉak karuti aṟivillār mayaṅkuvār.
iṟcārviṉ ēmāntēm īṅkamintēm eṉṟeṇṇip
poccān toḻukuvar pētiyār — accārvu
niṉṟaṉa pōṉṟu niliyā eṉavuṇarntār
eṉṟum pariva tilar.
Fools make their home in a changing world.
Fools thinking 'we have found joy in the refuge of home,' and 'we are
here in perfect repose, forgetful (of the instability of all things), go
on their way.
Those who have felt that 'refuge' is unstable, while it seems to stand
fast, never fix their fond desire (on it).
183. aṟiñar aṟampurintu vāḻvār
maṟumikku vittu mayaliṉṟic ceytu
ciṟumip paṭātēnīr vāḻmiṉ — aṟiñarāy
niṉṟuḻi niṉṟē niṟamvēṟāṅ kāraṇam
iṉṟip palavum uḷ.
Seek the unchanging. All things here change.
See that ye sow seed that in other world may germinate; free your lives
from earth's bewilder-ment and meanness ; stand in your lot as wise men;
the changing hue of things shall without cuse fade, and many things be
new.
184. periyōrpōlac ciṟiyōr aṟam ceyyār.
uṟipparuṅ kālattum ūṟṟunīrk kēṇi
iṟittuṇiṉum ūrāṟṟum eṉpar koṭikkaṭaṉuñ
cāayak kaṇṇum periyārpōṉ maṟṟiyār
āayak kaṇṇum aritu.
Good men are unfailing fountins of beneficence.
The well of springing water serves the town that draws and drinks, even
in time when rins are scant; so great men in adverse hour dispense due
gifts that others give not even in their best estate.
185. celvam keṭiṉum periyōr aṟañceytē vāḻvār.
uṟupuṉal tantula kūṭṭi aṟumiṭattum
kallūṟ ṟuḻiyūṟum āṟēpōṟ - celvam
palarkkāṟṟik keṭṭulantak kaṇṇuñ cilarkkāṟṟic
ceyvar ceyaṟpā lavi.
Great men do not neglect dutiesbecuse of poverty.
The river pours forth a mighty stream and feeds the world; and when it
is dried up, if men dig in its bed, streams gush out! So good men when
rich, give to many; and, when ruined, give still at least to some, and
do what should be done.
186. ciṟiyōr ceyyun tīmikaḷ teriyāmal maṟiyum.
peruvari nāṭa periyārkaṇ tīmi
karunarimēṟ cūṭēpōl tōṉṟum — karunariyik
koṉṟaṉṉa iṉṉā ceyiṉum ciṟiyārmēl
oṉṟāṉum tōṉṟā keṭum.
Faults are conspicuous in great persons.
Lord of the lofty hills! Any evil in men of moral greatness shows like a
brand on a mighty bull; but although mean men do pinful deeds, like the
slughter of that same mighty bull, none of these attracts attention.
187. periyōr pakiyiṉum ciṟiyōr naṭpē tīmi viḷikkum.
icinta ciṟumi iyalpilā tārkaṭ
pacinta tuṇiyum parivām — acinta
nakiyēyum vēṇṭāta nallaṟiviṉārkaṭ
pakiyēyum pāṭu peṟum.
Enmity of the wise better than friendship of the mean.
In proportion to the degree of one's intimacy with men essentially mean
and without good qualities sorrow accrues; but even the hostility of
those who do not desire forbidden things even in jest will confer
dignity.
188. poyyarukkup poyyākavum meyyarukku meyyākavum naṭappatu naṉṟu.
melliya nallāruḷ meṉmi atuviṟantu
oṉṉāruṭ kūṟṟukkum uṭkuṭimi ellāñ
calavaruṭ cālac calamē nalavaruḷ
naṉmi varampāy viṭal.
Be all things to all men.
With gentle fair ones use gentleness surpassing theirs; with foes
display a wrath that death's self might dread; with persistent men show
a persistency to match ; amongst men of good do good; let the law of
life be thus lid down.
189. periyōr puṟaṅkūṟṟik kēṭpiṉum maṉam vēṟupaṭār.
kaṭukki oruvaṉ kaṭuṅkuṟaḷi pēci
mayakki viṭiṉum maṉappirippoṉ ṟiṉṟit
tuḷakka milātavar tūya maṉattār
viḷakkiṉuḷ oṇcuṭarē pōṉṟu.
The great men is unmoved amid insults.
These are the pure in heart who, though any vex, and with use of
slanderous words would fain perplex them, with calm unruffled mind abide
unwavering, like the bright flame within the lamp.
190. vantavarkku uṇavu koṭuttup piṉ uṇṭu makiḻvār periyōr.
muṟṟuṟṟun tuṟṟiṉi nāḷum aṟañceytu
piṟṟuṟṟut tuṟṟuvar cāṉṟavar — attuṟṟu
mukkuṟṟam nīkki muṭiyum aḷavellām
tukkattuḷ nīkki viṭum.
Feeding the hungry.
The excellent will dily give to the needy in charity their first-served
food; they themselves will eat what is served after: such good conduct
removes the three faults, and from sorrow sets men free, through all the
days, till comes the end.
---------------

20. tāḷāṇmi / Ch. 20. Persevering Energy

191. muyaṟciyuṭiyavar mutaṉmiyāka vāḻvār.
kōḷāṟṟak koḷḷāk kuḷattiṉkīḻp piṅkūḻpōl
kēḷīva tuṇṭu kiḷikaḷō tuñcupa
vāḷāṭu kūttiyar kaṇpōl taṭumāṟum
tāḷāḷark kuṇṭō tavaṟu.
Active independence.
As for kindred that feed upon what relatives give them they will die off
like green grin below a tank, which does not hold a sufficient supply
(of water).
Is failure possible to men of energy that are quick in movement as the
eyes of those who perform the sword-dance ?
192. tāḻvilā muyaṟci talivaṉākac ceyyum.
āṭukō ṭāki atariṭi niṉṟatūum
kāḻ koṇṭa kaṇṇē kaḷiṟaṇikkuṅ kantākum;
vāḻtalum aṉṉa takittē oruvaṉṟāṉ
tāḻaviṉṟit taṉṉic ceyiṉ.
By energy a man makes himself.
What once stood by the wayside, a twig that bent to every touch, when
its core is developed within, may become a stake to which they tie an
elephant. Life too is thus, if man himself, unfailing make himself !
193. muyaṟciyuṭivaṉukkuc ciṟutoḻilum peruntoḻilām.
uṟupuli ūṉiri iṉṟi orunāḷ
ciṟutēri paṟṟiyum tiṉṉum — aṟiviṉāl
kāltoḻil eṉṟu karutaṟka kiyiṉāl
mēltoḻilum āṅkē mikum.
Stoop to conquer.
The huge tiger, when lacking flesh for food one day, may even seize a
little frog and eat it. Think not 'By (all my) knowledge I only gin
menial tasks;" to the skillful hand nobler employments shall even there
abound.
194. cōrvu iṉṟit toḻil purivatutāṉ āṇmi.
iciyā teṉiṉum iyaṟṟiyōr āṟṟāl
aciyātu niṟpatām āṇmi — iciyuṅkāl
kaṇṭal tiriyalikkum kāṉalam taṇcērppa
peṇṭirum vāḻārō maṟṟu.
Manliness is perseverance in spite of failure.
Manliness is working on, in no wise faltering, remining stedfast though
the matter succeed not. When all is successful,— Lord of the cool lovely
shore, in whose groves the waves agitate the scented thorn, — will not
even women live and flourish?
195 kalvi, celvam, tavam, muyaṟci iviyiruntāl uyarkulam ākum.
nalla kulameṉṟum tīya kulameṉṟum
collaḷa vallāl poruḷilli — tolciṟappiṉ
oṇporuḷ oṉṟō tavamkalvi āḷviṉi
eṉṟivaṟṟāṉ ākuṅ kulam.
What is good or bad caste ?
When men speak of 'good caste' and 'bad caste' it is a mere form of
speech, and has no real meaning. Not even by possessions, made splendid
by ancient glories, but by self-denial, learning, and energy is Caste
determined.
196. muyaṟciyi muṭiviṟkāṭṭuvōrē mutaṉmiyuṭiyavar.
āṟṟun tuṇiyum aṟiviṉi uḷḷaṭakki
ūkkam uriyār uṇarvuṭiyār — ūkkam
uṟuppiṉāl ārāyum oṇmi yuṭiyār
kuṟippiṉkīḻp paṭṭa tulaku.
Reserve as to your own plans, and skill in detecting those of others.
Till the time for action comes men of understanding keep close within
themselves their wisdom and speak not of their designs (ūkkam = What they
are labouring to effect).
The world is subject to the nod of the brilliant (diplomatists) who
search out (and know) men's designs from outward indications (lit. from)
their members, i.e. from eye, gesture, tone, expression etc.).
197. tanti taḷarntāl mintaṉ tāṅkavēṇṭum.
citali tiṉappaṭṭa āla maratti
mataliyāy maṟṟataṉ vīrūṉṟi yāṅkuk
kutalimi tantikaṇ tōṉṟiltāṉ peṟṟa
putalvaṉ maṟippak keṭum.
The worthy son conceals his sire's defects, and sustins his weakness.
If the banyan's trunk be eaten by the gnawing ant, its 'branch root'
bears it up, like a buttress; even so, when decay appears in the sire,
the son he has begotten shall hide it, and weakness is no more.
198. aḻikkattakka ceyalip paṇpuṭiyōr ceyyār.
īṉamāy illirun tiṉṟi viḷiyiṉum
māṉan talivaruva ceypavō — yāṉi
varimukam puṇpaṭukkum vaḷḷukir nōṉṟāḷ
arimā matuki yavar.
Poverty but not dishonour.
The lion's pointed claw and mighty foot will wound the spotted face of
an elephant; those who have power like his , — though bereft of all,
they die in want within their home, — will they do deeds that bring
disgrace?
199. pērāṇmi iṉṟeṉiṟ piṟappāṟ payaṉilli.
tīṅkarum pīṉṟa tiraḷkāl uḷiyalari
tēṅkamaḻ nāṟṟam iḻantāraṅku — ōṅkum
uyarkuṭi yuṭpiṟappi ṉeṉṉām peyarpoṟikkum
pērāṇmi yillāk kaṭi.
High birth useless without lofty energies.
Like the flowers on a rounded stalk, with hir like filaments that sweet
cane bare, when the sweet fragrance they breathed is lost, what gin
accrues from birth in a lofty noble house when energy that makes the
name noteworthy, is wanting?
200. uḻippāṟ kiṭitta uṇavē uyarntatu.
perumut tariyar perituvan tīyuṅ
karuṉiccō ṟārvar kayavar — karuṉiyip
pērum aṟiyār naṉivirumpu tāḷāṇmi
nīrum amiḻtāy viṭum.
The scant fare of the laborious is the diet of the gods.
The base feed full of rice and savoury food, that men, great lords of
the triple lands, with generous gladness give; but water won with
willing strenuous toil by those who know, not savoury food by name even,
will turn to nectar.
-------------

21. cuṟṟantaḻāl / Ch. 21. The Support of Kindred

201. cuṟṟattārik kaṇṭāl tuṉpam nīṅkum.
vayāvum varuttamum īṉṟakkāl nōvum
kavāaṉ makaṟkaṇṭu tāymaṟan tāaṅku
acāattāṉ uṟṟa varuttam ucāattaṉ
kēḷirik kāṇak keṭum.
Comfort from sympathy of kinsmen.
As a mother when she sees her son upon her lap forgets the languors, the
pins, and the throes of birth; so trouble arising from weakness will
die when a man sees his sympathizing kinsmen around him.
202. pallār payaṉpeṟa nallāṇmakaṉ vāḻvāṉ.
aḻalmaṇṭu pōḻtiṉ aṭintavarkaṭ kellām
niḻalmarampōl nēroppat tāṅkip — paḻumarampōl
pallār payaṉtuyppat tāṉvarunti vāḻvatē
nallāṇ makaṟkuk kaṭaṉ.
Good friends like trees that afford both shade and fruit.
To yield ready protection alike to all, as a tree affords shade to those
that seek its shelter when the heat grows fierce; and to live toiling so
that many may enjoy the gin, resembling thus a fruit producing tree, is
the duty of the manly man.
203. taṟcērntavarit tāṅkuvar periyōr.
aṭukkal malināṭa taṟcērn tavari
eṭukkalām eṉṟār periyōr — aṭuttaṭuttu
vaṉkāy palapala kāyppiṉum illiyē
taṉkāy poṟukkalāk kompu.
The magnanimous never refuse to support their kindred. The bough
sustins all its fruits.
Lord of the land where mountins piled on mountins rise! the great
demur not to support their kith and kin ;—there is no bough but will
support the fruit it bears, though clustered thick great fruits and many
cling thereon.
204. ciṟṟiṉattār kēṇmi cilanāḷ niṟkum.
ulakaṟiyat tīrak kalappiṉum nillā
cilapakalāñ ciṟṟiṉattār kēṇmi — nilitiriyā
niṟkum periyōr neṟiyaṭiya niṉṟaṉittāl
oṟka milāḷar toṭarpu.
The friendship of the great alone is lasting.
Though mingled in a complete intimacy so that all the world knows of it,
the friendship of the little will last but little time. Connection with
the firm unyielding men endures till the great one's path, who never
swerve, is reached.
205. palartuyar tīrppavar talivar āvār.
iṉṉar iṉiyar emarpiṟar eṉṉuñcol
eṉṉum ilarām iyalpiṉāl — tuṉṉit
tolimakkaḷ tuṉpan tīrp pārēyār māṭṭum
talimakka ḷākaṟpā lār.
Universal benevolence.
‘Such are they and so many;’ ‘these are ours; those strange :’ those
worthy to be classed as chief of men say nothing like this ; so to speak
is not their nature ; for they relieve the distress of ALL that troubled
come to them!
206. āruyir naṇpartarum kūḻum amutamām.
poṟkalattup peyta puliyukir vāṉpuḻukkal
akkāram pālō ṭamarārkit tuṇṭaliṉ
uppilip puṟki uyirpōṟ kiḷiñarmāṭṭu
ekkālat tāṉum iṉitu.
Hard fare with kindred, better than a feast without love.
More sweet than rice, though white as tiger's claw with milk and sugar
served on plate of gold, by loveless hands, is any tasteless mess, in
any dish, when shared with kindred dear as life.
207. pakivar maṉiyiṟ pāṟcōṟum vēmpām.
nāḷvāyp peṟiṉuntan naḷḷātōr illattu
vēḷāṇmi veṅkaruṉi vēmpākum — kēḷāy
aparāṇap pōḻtiṉ aṭakiṭuva rēṉun
tamarāyār māṭṭē iṉitu.
Any food with foes bitter, with friends sweet.
Most bitter (Margosa) is the bounteous meal of dinty food at early dawn
in house of those who love us not. Hear thou! though not till evening
given, the mess of herbs when eaten with our own is sweet.
208. uṟṟaviṭat tutavuvōr naṭṭava reṉpār.
muṭṭiki pōla muṉiyātu vikalum
koṭṭiyuṇ pāruṅ kuṟaṭupōṟ kiviṭuvar
cuṭṭukkōl pōla eriyum pukuvarē
naṭṭā reṉappaṭu vār.
Interested and disinterested friends.
Even those who, like the artificer's small hammer (with slight strokes
fashioning the jewel), gently (muṉiyātu), day by day, moulding their
patron to their will, eat his food, will drop him (when poverty assils
him), as the pincers do the gold put into the crucible. Those worthy to
be called friends are like the artificer's rod which enters the fire
with it.
209. iṭar makiḻ vāruṅkē eytuvar naṇpar.
naṟumalart taṇkōtāy! naṭṭārkku naṭṭār
maṟumiyuñ ceyvatoṉ ṟuṇṭō — iṟumaḷavum
iṉpuṟuva iṉpuṟ ṟeḻīi avarōṭu
tuṉpuṟuva tuṉpuṟāk kāl.
Sympathy in sorrow and in joy.
O mid adorned with fresh garlands of fragrant flowers! is there one
thing that even in other world, friends may perform for friends, if till
they die, their joys they share, but shun to share their griefs ?
210. viruppamillār maṉip porikkaṟiyuṇavum vēmpākum.
viruppilā rillattu vēṟirun tuṇṇum
verukkukkaṇ veṅkaruṉi vēmpām — viruppuṭit
taṉpōlvā rilluḷa tayaṅkunīrt taṇpuṟki
eṉpō ṭiyinta amiḻtu.
Better fast with friends than feast with foes.
The savoury fried curry, (in colour) like a cat's eye, which one eats
seated a part in the house of those who are without affection, will be
bitter as margosa; but cold gruel (weak and insipid), like clear water,
in the house of affectionate equals, is ambrosia that cleaves to the
bones.
--------------

22. naṭpārāytal/Ch. 22. Scrutiny in Forming Friendships

211. nāḷukku nāḷ uyarum nallōr naṭpu.
karuttuṇarntu kaṟṟaṟintār kēṇmiyeñ ñāṉṟum
kuruttiṟ karumputiṉ ṟaṟṟē — kuruttiṟku
etircelattiṉ ṟaṉṉa takittarō eṉṟum
matura milāḷar toṭarpu.
Satisfying friendships.
Intimacy with those who understand the real intention (of one's words),
and who have acquired wisdom by learning, will at all times be like
eating sweet cane from the tender shoots; but attachment to those who
have no sweetness of disposition is like eating it in a direction
opposite to the tender shoot (it grows harder and less sweet).
212. kuṭip piṟappaṟintē naṭpuk koḷka.
iṟpiṟap peṇṇi iṭitiriyār eṉpatōr
naṟpuṭi koṇṭami yallatu — poṟkēḻ
puṉaloḻukap puḷḷiriyum pūṅkuṉṟa nāṭa!
maṉamaṟiyap paṭṭatoṉ ṟaṉṟu.
Examine the lineage of a candidate for your friendship.
Lord of the land of flowery hills, where wild-fowl golden in hue fly,
scared by the rush of the waterfall.
Regarding the mobility of their birth—these will not swerve:—To say thus
is a good ground of confidence—(a good position to take up); but to say
‘their minds are known,' is not any (i. e. real ground).
213. nāy pōṉṟavarē naṭpiṟ ciṟantavar.
yāṉi aṉiyavar naṇporīi nāyaṉiyār
kēṇmi keḻīik koḷalvēṇṭum — yāṉi
aṟintaṟintum pākaṉiyē kollum eṟintavēl
meyyatā vālkuḻikkum nāy.
The elephant and the dog, types of false and true friends.
Forsaking friendship with those who resemble the elephant, embrace and
hold fast intimacy with those who are like the dog; for the elephant
will slay even its keeper though it has long known him ; but the dog
will wag its til when it has in its body the javelin (hurled at it by
its angry master).
214. uḷḷaṅ kalantavar urimi naṇpuṭiyavar.
palanāḷum pakkattā rāyiṉum neñciṟ
cilanāḷum oṭṭārō ṭoṭṭār — palanāḷum
nīttā reṉakki viṭaluṇṭō tanneñcattu
yāttārō ṭiyātta toṭarpu.
Friends are not to be forsaken becuse long severed
Though men dwell side by side for many days when their souls cleave not
(are not congenial) for even a few days they retin not their
friendship. But is it possible to let go attachment's well-knit ties,
though those to whom one's soul is knit dwell many days afar?
215. curuṅkā tirukkum ciṟantavar naṭpu.
kōṭṭuppūp pōl malarntupiṉ kūmpātu
vēṭṭatē vēṭṭatām naṭpāṭci — tōṭṭa
kayappūppōl muṉmalarntu piṉkūmpu vāri
nayappārum naṭpārum il.
Tree-flowers and water-flowers.
The use and wont of friendship is that, what once it has loved it loves
always,— like the flower on the tree-branch which having once unfolded
afterwards closes not; but who will esteem, or make friends of those who
are like flowers on the surface of the excavated tank, which unfold and
afterwards close themselves?
216. kamuku teṉṉi paṉi pōṉṟatu kaṭiyiṭi mutal naṭpu.
kaṭiyāyār naṭpiṟ kamukaṉiyar ēṉi
iṭiyāyār teṅkiṉ aṉiyar — taliyāyār
eṇṇarum peṇṇipōṉ ṟiṭṭañāṉ ṟiṭṭatē
toṉmi uṭiyār toṭarpu.
Three grades of friends.
The lowest sort of men in friendship are like the Arecanut tree. The
middle sort are like the cocoa-nut tree. Attachments to the chief of men
— who are old friends —is like the Palmyra tree of rare worth : what was
given that first day was given once for all.
217. aṉpilār tanta amutum eṭṭikkāy.
kaḻunīruṭ kāraṭa kēṉum oruvaṉ
viḻumitāk koḷḷiṉ amiḻtam — viḻumiya
kuyttuviyār veṇcōṟē yāyiṉum mēvātār
kittuṇṭal kāñciraṅ kāy.
A dinner of herbs with affection is ambrosia. The greatest delicacies
without it, nux vomica.
If one receive you courteously, though what he gives is but rank herbs
dressed in water in which rice had been washed, it is ambrosia. To eat
from the hands of those who love us not, though it be white rice with
rich spicy condiments, is nux vomica.
218. vāykkāl pōṉṟavar naṭpi maruvuka.
nāykkāṟ ciṟuviralpōl naṉkaṇiya rāyiṉum
īkkāl tuṇiyum utavātār naṭpeṉṉām ?
cēyttāṉum ceṉṟu koḷalvēṇṭum ceyviḷikkum
vāykkālaṉiyār toṭarpu.
Friendship of those who though near aid not.
Of what value is the friendship of those who being very near like the
little claw on a dog's leg, afford not help as much as a fly's foot?
Though you go far to seek it, lay hold of the friendship of those who
are like the water channel that cuses the crops in the field to
flourish.
219. teḷivilār naṭpukkoḷvatu tītē.
teḷivilār naṭpiṟ pakinaṉṟu cātal
viḷiyā arunōyiṉ naṉṟāl — aḷiya
ikaḻtaliṉ kōṟal iṉitēmaṟ ṟilla
pukaḻtaliṉ vitalē naṉṟu.
Four bad things.
Better hate than friendship of the ignorant. Better death than disease
which comes on yielding to no remedy. Sweeter is killing thin contempt
that breaks the spirit. Better abuse than undeserved prise.
220. takkavar naṭpiṉi āyntu cārntiru.
marīip palarōṭu paṉṉāḷ muyaṅkip
porīip poruṭṭakkārk kōṭalē vēṇṭum ;
parīi uyircekukkum pāmpoṭum iṉṉā
marīiip piṉṉip pirivu.
Never forsake a friend !
When men have formed an intimacy (marīi) separation afterwards even from a
snake which slays with its tooth (parīi), cuses affliction (iṉṉā)
therefore associate intimately with many, and for many days take them to
your bosom, conform to their tastes and habits (porīi ), and hold fast
the really worthy ones.
-----------

23. naṭpiṟ piḻi poṟuttal / Ch. 23. Bearing and Forbearing in Friendship

221. naṭpiṉar piḻiyi nallōr poṟuppar.
nallār eṉattām naṉivirumpik koṇṭāri
allār eṉiṉum aṭakkik koḷalvēṇṭum;
nelluk kumiyuṇṭu nīrkku nuriyuṇṭu
pullitaḻ pūviṟku muṇṭu.
Bear with infirmities. None are perfect.
When those to whom with strong desire we clung as good, prove otherwise,
keep the sad secret bid, - cling to them still! The growing grin has
husks; the water has its foam: flowers too have scentless outer sheath
of leaves.
222. veṟuppaṉa ceyyiṉum poṟuppar periyōr.
ceṟuttō ṟuṭippiṉuñ cempuṉalō ṭūṭār
maṟuttuñ ciṟiceyvar nīrnacii vāḻnar
veṟuppa veṟuppac ceyiṉum poṟupparē
tāmvēṇṭik koṇṭār toṭarpu.
Bear with your friends' faults, as the cultivator bears with the stream
that often bursts its enclosure.
If though they dam it up, the fresh flood should burst its bonds, men do
not feel aggrieved; but strightway imprison it agin, for by the
precious stream they live: so though their friends agin and agin do
very disagreeable things, men bear with those whose friendship is dear.
223. oruvar poṟumiyē iruvar naṭpu ākum.
iṟappavē tīya ceyiṉuntam naṭṭār
poṟuttal takuvatoṉ ṟaṉṟō — niṟakkōṅku
uruvavaṇ ṭārkkum uyarvari nāṭa!
oruvar poṟiyiruvar naṭpu.
Bear all things!
Lord of the lofty hilly land, where the bees hum through all the flowery
Gongu-glade ! — Though friends should work us surpassing ills, the only
thing that is meet is forbearance: patience of one is friendship of the
twain.
224. naṭpiṉar pirivē uṭcuṭum tīyām.
maṭitiri tantiṭṭa vāṉkatir muttam
kaṭuvici nāvāy kariyalikkuñ cērppa!
viṭutaṟ kariyār iyalpilarēl neñcam
cuṭutaṟku mūṭṭiya tī.
To be wroth with those we love, is like fire in the breast!
Lord of the shore where pearls of purest lustre are thrown up by
circling waves, and where swift darting boats are borne through the surf
!—when friends who we may not leave have alien hearts, it is as a
scorching fire enkindled in the soul.
225. naṉmi naṭpiṉarip poṉṉeṉap pōṟṟuka.
iṉṉā ceyiṉum viṭaṟpāla rallārip
poṉṉākap pōṟṟik koḷalvēṇṭum — poṉṉoṭu
nalliṟ citittatī nāṭoṟum nāṭittam
illattil ākkuta lāl.
Forsake not friends though they wrong you.
Though those, from whom you may not part, do grievous things, O mid who
art as Lakshmi! you must still cherish your chosen friends—fire destroys
men's wealth and happy homes, yet is it sought there and kindled every
day.
226. tuṉpañ ceyyiṉum naṇparit taḻuvuka.
iṉṉā ceyiṉum viṭutaṟ kariyārit
tuṉṉāt tuṟattal takuvatō — tuṉṉaruñcīr
viṇkuttu nīḷvari veṟpa! kaḷipavō
kaṇkuttiṟ ṟeṉṟutaṅ ki.
Friends are not to be forsaken on account of their faults.
When those from whom it is hard to part do evil things should men at
once renounce them?—Lord of the lengthening hills that pierce the sky,
whence rarest giftsdescend !—Do men cut off their hand becuse it
pricked their eye?
227. naṭṭapiṉ tīmi nāṭutal naṉṟala.
ilaṅkunīrt taṇcērppa! iṉṉā ceyiṉum
kalantu paḻikāṇār cāṉṟōr — kalantapiṉ
tīmi yeṭutturikkun tiṇṇaṟivillātār
tāmum avariṟ kaṭi.
Those who forsake friends that have committed a fault are worse than
they.
Lord of the cool shore of the shining sea! The perfect ones when they
have contracted an intimacy with any, see no faults in them, even if
they do things that cuse pin. Those who, being without stable wisdom,
take up and tell out men's evil deeds after contracting an intimacy with
them, are themselves worse than they.
228. naṇparkaḷ ceyalellām āyntāl naṉmiyām.
ētilār ceyta tiṟappavē tīteṉiṉum
nōtakka teṉṉuṇṭām nōkkuṅkāl — kātal
kaḻumiyār ceyta kaṟaṅkaruvi nāṭa!
viḻumitām neñcattuḷ niṉṟu.
Faults in strangers and in friends
Lord of the land of resounding waterfalls ! Though what those alien to
us have done may be surpassingly evil, what is there to be pined at,
when you regard it?
Things done by affectionate friends, will be excellent when so regarded
by the mind.
229. vēṟṟumi naṇpiyum tūṟṟātu aṭakkuka.
tamareṉṟu tāṅkoḷḷap paṭṭavar tammit
tamaraṉmi tāmaṟintā rāyiṉ avarit
tamariṉum naṉku matittut tamaraṉmi
tammuḷ aṭakkik koḷal.
If friends prove unfaithful, love them the more, and keep the secret of
their unfaithfulness in your own bosom.
If we perceive those we have accepted as our own to be not really ours,
we must pay even more respect to them than to our own, and keep
concealed in our own mind the fact that they are not really ours.
230. naṭpiṭik kuṟṟam nāṭutal tītu.
kuṟṟamum ēṉik kuṇamum oruvaṉi
naṭṭapiṉ nāṭit tirivēṉēl — naṭṭāṉ
maṟikāvā viṭṭavaṉ celvuḻic celka
aṟikaṭalcūḻ viyam naka.
He who pries into his friends faults shares the punishment of the
revealer of secrets. If, after I have taken a man for my friend, I go
about prying into his faults and virtues (other qualities), may I
departwhither that man goes who has not kept his friend's secret, while
the earth, begirt by the resounding sea, lughs.
--------------

24. kūṭā naṭpu / Ch. 24. Unreal Friendship

231. karumam muṟṟuntuṇi kayavarnaṭpu niṟkum.
ceṟippil paḻaṅkūri cēṟaṇi yāka
iṟittunīr ēṟṟuṅ kiṭappar —kaṟikkuṉṟam
poṅkaruvi tāḻum puṉalvari naṉṉāṭa!
taṅkarum muṟṟun tuṇi.
Unreal friends cling to you till they have gined their desire.
Lord of the pleasant well-watered mountin land, where boiling
waterfalls pour down from the dark hills! (Poor men) linger beneath the
old roof that affords no shelter, baling out the water, and making mud
embankments, and enduring the down-pour; (even so unreal friends stick
by you) till their purpose has been attined.
232. māri peyyāmiām cīrilār naṭpu.
cīriyār kēṇmi ciṟanta ciṟappiṟṟāy
māripōl māṇṭa payattatām — māri
vaṟantakkāl pōlumē vālaruvi nāṭa!
ciṟantakkāṟ cīrilār naṭpu.
The friendship of the excellent like rin ; of others like drought.
Lord of the land of pure (white, forming) waterfalls! The friendship of
the virtuous is of exceeding excellence, and yields glorious results —
like (seasonable) rin; but the friendship of the vicious, even in the
time of its exuberance, is as when the rin fails in the time of
drought.
233. uṇarvilā naṭpu nirayatti ottatē.
nuṇṇuṇarvi ṉāroṭu kūṭi nukarvuṭimi
viṇṇulakē yokkum viḻiviṟṟām — nuṇṇūl
uṇarvila rākiya ūtiya millārp
puṇartal nirayattuḷ oṉṟu.
Bliss enjoyed with the wise is heaven; association with the ignorant and
worthless is hell.
Enjoyment of the society of men of refinement resembles the heavenly
world in the pleasure it affords. Closest contact with those destitute
of fine perception of the value of learned pursuits —men who gin no
wisdom from you, nor you from them—is one of the hells.
234. kūṭānaṭpup perukip piṉ kuṟiyum.
perukuvatu pōlat tōṉṟivit tīppōl
orupoḻutuñ cellātē nantum — arukellāñ
cantaṉa nīḷcōlic cāral malināṭa!
pantamilāḷar toṭarpu.
Intimacy with those who have no sympathy is like fire in the stubble.
Lord of the land where wide groves of Sandal cover the hilly slopes!
Friendship with those who feel not its real obligation, like fire in the
straw (suddenly) appears, seeming as though it would increase, but never
advancing dies out.
235. kūṭā naṭput tuṉpamē koṭukkum.
ceyyāta ceytumnām eṉṟālum ceytavaṉic
ceyyātu tāḻttukkoṇ ṭōṭṭalum — meyyāka
iṉpuṟūum peṟṟi ikaḻntārkkum anniliyē
tuṉpuṟūum peṟṟi tarum.
Boastings and delays.
The promising to do what cannot be done, and the putting off and leaving
undone through delay things that could be done, will forthwith bring
experience of sorrow even to those who have condemned truthfully the
pleasant experiences of life (ascetics and sints).
236. ciṟiyavari aṟiyalām ceyalvēṟupāṭṭiṉāl.
orunīrp piṟantōruṅku nīṇṭak kaṭittum
virinīrk kuvaḷiyi āmpalok kallā ;
perunīrār kēṇmi koḷiṉunī rallār
karumaṅkaḷ vēṟu paṭum.
The water-lily does not become a lotus by being in the same tank with
it: so evil persons will act in conformity with their natures.
The Ambal (water-lily) does not equalthe expanding Kuvali though born
and growing together with it in the same pool : though they attin to
intimacy with those of generous instincts, the deeds of men in whom
these instincts are lacking will be diverse.
237. oṟṟumiyillār naṭpu iṉṉātu.
muṟṟaṟ ciṟumanti muṟpaṭṭa tantiyi
neṟṟukkaṇ ṭaṉṉa viralāl ñemirttiṭṭuk
kuṟṟip paṟikkum malināṭa! iṉṉātē
oṟṟumi koḷḷātār naṭpu.
Friendship with the uncongenial is bad.
Lord of the hilly land where the immature little monkey, with its finger
like a bean-pod, will flip its father when it meets him, and poke him
and snatch fruit from him! Afflictive indeed is friendship with the
uncongenial.
238. uṟṟaviṭat tutavuvatē uyarnta naṭpākum.
muṭṭuṟṟa pōḻtiṉ muṭukieṉ āruyiri
naṭṭāṉ oruvaṉki nīṭṭēṉēl — naṭṭāṉ
kaṭimaṉi kaṭṭaḻittāṉ celvuḻic celka
neṭumoḻi viyam naka.
The curse of him who does not offer his life for his friend.
If I hasten not to put forth my hand and offer my precious life to my
friend when in distress, may I depart whither he goes who has violated
the sanctity of his friend's wedded wife, while the far-famed world
lughs!
239. pullaṟivuṭiyār naṭpup poruntātu.
āṉpaṭu neypey kalaṉuḷ atukaḷintu
vēmpaṭu neypey taṉittarō ; —tēmpaṭu
nalvari nāṭa! nayamuṇarvār naṇporīip
pullaṟivi ṉāroṭu naṭpu.
Bitter for sweet.
Lord of the land of goodly hills where honey flows! To forsake the
friendship of those who know the right, and cultivate that of shallow
pretenders to knowledge, is like emptying out cow's ghee from a vessel
and pouring into it margosa oil.
240. tīyiṉattāric cērvatu tīmiyām.
uruviṟ kamintāṉkaṇ ūrāṇmi iṉmi
parukaṟ kamintapāl nīraḷā yaṟṟē;
terivuṭiyār tīyiṉattā rākutal nākam
viripeṭiyō ṭāṭiviṭ ṭaṟṟu.
A specious outward appearance without a liberal spirit
The absence of generosity in those whose exterior is pleasing, is like
the mingling of water with the milk provided for food: when men of
understanding take to bad company it is like the disporting of a Cobra
with a female viper.
-------------

25. aṟivuṭimi / Ch. 25. The Possession of Practical Wisdom

241. taḷarntāṟ pakivarit tāṅkuvar mēlōr.
pakivar paṇiviṭam nōkkit takavuṭiyār
tāmēyum nāṇit taliccellār kāṇāy;
iḷampiṟi yāyakkāl tiṅkaḷic cērā(tu)
aṇaṅkarun tuppiṉ arā.
Generosity to fallen foes.
Worthy men, when they behold where foes are foiled, themselves too feel
sore abashed, and do not hasten on to crush them. Behold, the strong
invulnerable dragon draws not near the moon (to swallow it) when it is
in its tender crescent days!
242. aṭakkamē makkaṭku aṇikalam āvatu.
naḷikaṭal taṇcērppa! nalkūrnta makkaṭ
kaṇikala māva taṭakkam — paṇivilcīr
māttiri yiṉṟi naṭakkumēl vāḻumūr
kōttiraṅ kūṟap paṭum.
Self-restrint, an ornament.
Lord of the cool shore of the spreading sea ! To men in poverty a modest
self-restrint is the chiefest ornament. If a man live in unbending
pride, and in a manner unbefitting his position, his fellow-townsmen
will revile his race.
243. maṟumiyiṉpamum tarum nalloḻukkam.
ennilattu vittiṭiṉum kāñciraṅkāḻ teṅkākā(tu)
ennāṭ ṭavarum cuvarkkam pukutalāl,
taṉṉāṟṟāṉ ākum maṟumi vaṭaticiyum
koṉṉāḷar cālap palar.
Character, not birthplace.
Whatever soil you sow it in, the Strychnos nut grows not into a
cocoa-palm. Some of the Southern land have entered Paradise! It is man's
way of life that decides his future state. Full many from the Northern
land are denizens of hell.
244. iṉattāl mēlōr maṉam māṟupaṭātu.
vēmpiṉ iliyuṭ kaṉiyiṉum vāḻitaṉ
tīñcuvi yātun tiriyātām; āṅkē
iṉantī teṉiṉum iyalpuṭiyār kēṇmi
maṉantītām pakkam aritu.
Good men not affected by corrupt influences.
Though ripened amid margosa leaves the fruit of the plantin loses no
atom of its sweet flavour. Even so the friendship of men of noble mood,
although their race be evil, can hardly work ill to the mind.
245. iyaṟki yaṟivuṭiyavar eṉṟum māṟupaṭār.
kaṭalcārntum iṉṉīr piṟakkum, malicārntum
uppīṇ ṭuvari piṟattalāl ; tattam
iṉattaṉiya rallar eṟikaṭaltaṇcērppa!
maṉattaṉiyar makkaḷeṉ pār.
Not environment, but mind makes the man.
Though close by the sea, sweet waters oft-times spring up there; on the
hill-side the waters often gush out all brine! Thus men are not as their
race. — Lord of the dashing sea's cool shore! Men are as their minds.
246. kūṭutal pirital cāṉṟōr kuṟittiṭār.
parāarip puṉṉi paṭukaṭal taṇ cērppa!
orāalum oṭṭaluñ ceypavō? nalla
marūuccey tiyārmāṭṭut taṅku maṉattār
virāaayac ceyyāmi naṉṟu.
Aginst caprice.
Lord of the cool sea-shore, where flourishes the thick-stemmed lurel !
Men whose minds are good (constant), and who adhere to whomsoever they
have formed an intimacy with, will not sometimes avoid men, and at other
times be intimate. It is good not to have fits of alternating warmth and
indifference.
247. aṟiñaric cārntāl iṉpam aṇukum
uṇara uṇarum uṇarvuṭi yārip
puṇariṟ puṇarumām. iṉpam ;—puṇariṉ
teriyat teriyun terivilā tārip
piriyap piriyumām nōy.
Good and bad associations.
Join the men who throughly feel true wisdom's inner sense, and forthwith
joy joins you. Join yourself to men devoid of the accurate preception of
knowledge, and then parting from them is parting from pin.
248. tāṉē taṉṉit talivaṉ ākkalām.
naṉṉilikkaṇ taṉṉi niṟuppāṉum, taṉṉi
nilikalakkik kīḻiṭu vāṉum, —niliyiṉum
mēṉmēl uyarttu niṟuppāṉum, taṉṉit
taliyākac ceyvāṉum tāṉ.
Men makes, unmakes, and ennobles himself.
He that establishes a man in good, and he that disturbs that good
position and casts him down, and he that more and more exalts a man and
establishes him, and he that makes a man head (among men) is (the MAN)
HIMSELF.
249. kāriyam muṭikkak kallār piṉṉum kaṟṟavar celvār.
karuma variciyāṟ kallātār piṉṉum
perumi yuṭiyāruñ cēṟal — arumarapiṉ
ōtam araṟṟum olikaṭal taṇcērppa!
pētimi yaṉṟa taṟivu.
It is prudent sometimes to sacrifice pride.
Lord of the cool shore of the sounding sea, where from old time the
billows roar!— In the course of their affairs when even great men follow
after the unlearned, this is not folly but wisdom.
250. karumam pōkam tarumam mūṉṟum takkōr aṭivar.
karumamum uṭpaṭap pōkamum tuvvat
tarumamum takkārkkē ceyyā — oruniliyē
muṭṭiṉṟi mūṉṟu muṭiyumēl aḵteṉpa
paṭṭiṉam peṟṟa kalam.
A perfect life-voyage.
If a man has wrought all fitting works, enjoyed all seemly pleasures,
done deeds of charity to worthy men: if he shall have accomplished all
these three unchecked, in this one state, of him men will say: ‘that is
a ship that has gined the haren'
-------------

26. aṟiviṉmi / Ch. 26. The Lack of Practical Wisdom

251. aṟivē peruñcelvam ; aṟiviṉmi vaṟumiyām.
nuṇṇuṇar viṉmi vaṟumi, aḵtuṭimi
paṇṇap paṇitta peruñcelvam ;—eṇṇuṅkāl
peṇavāy āṇiḻanta pēṭi aṇiyāḷō,
kaṇavāt takka kalam.
Lack of accurate perception is poverty. Mere externals are nothing.
The want of refined knowledge is poverty, its possession is very great
and abundant wealth. When one considers, will not a sexless creature,
more woman than man, adorn herself with the jewels that her eye desires
?
252. celvamum aṟivum ōriṭam cērā.
pallāṉṟa kēḷvip payaṉuṇarvār pāṭaḻintu
allal uḻappa taṟitirēl — tolciṟappiṉ
nāviṉ kiḻatti uṟitalāṟ cērāḷē,
pūviṉ kiḻatti pulantu.
Why the goddess Fortuna avoids the learned.
Men of vast and varied lore are seen in low estate, and suffer want.
Would you know the reason? The anciently renowned ‘Lady of the tongue'
abides with them). ‘The Lady of the flowers' is jealous, and draws not
near !
253. iḷamiyiṟ kallāṉ iḻivupiṉ eytuvāṉ.
kalleṉṟu tanti kaḻaṟa ataṉiyōr
colleṉṟu koḷḷā tikaḻntavaṉ , —mella
eḻuttōli pallārmuṉ nīṭṭa viḷiyā
vaḻukkōlik koṇṭu viṭum.
Folly of refusing to learn in youth.
He who, when his father urgently bade him learn, did not take it as a
serious matter, but contemned it; when, before many men, some one gently
presents a written palm-leaf, will in anger fetch a stick to beat him as
guilty of an insult.
254. kallātavaṉi nāy eṉṟē karutuvar.
kallātu nīṇṭa oruvaṉ ulakattu
nallaṟi vāḷa riṭippukku — mella
iruppiṉum nāyirun taṟṟē, irāa(tu)
urippiṉum nāykurit taṟṟu.
An ignorant man is a mere cur!
When a man who has grown up without learning enters the society of the
wise, if he sit still, it is as if a dog sat there; and if he rise to
speak, it is as though a dog barked.
255. pullar aviyiṟ kallār pukuvar.
pullāppuṉ kōṭṭip pulava riṭippukkuk
kallāta collum kaṭiyellām ;—kaṟṟa
kaṭāayiṉum cāṉṟavar collār poruḷmēṟ
paṭāa viṭupāk kaṟintu.
Cast not pearls before swine.
All the baser sort consorting with scholars of a heterodox and low
school, will utter illiterate rubbish; but men replete with learning,
though urgently asked, utter not the results of their learning, knowing
that (the asker) would fail to apply their minds to the import of what
was sid.
256. kallār virivuri yellām pakarvar.
kaṟṟaṟinta nāviṉār collārtam cōrvañci;
maṟṟiya rāvār pakarvar ; paṉiyiṉmēl
vaṟṟiya ōli kalakalakkum; eññāṉṟum
paccōlik killi oli.
Modest silence.
Men of learned tongues are silent, fearing some slip; others (ignorant
men) will speak out; on the Palmyra tree the dried-up leaves make a loud
rustling noise ; but evermore the green leaf gives forth no sound !
257. aṟivilār ceviyil aṟavuri ēṟātu.
paṉṟikkūḻp pattaril tēmā vaṭittaṟṟāl
naṉṟaṟiyā māntark kaṟattā ṟurikkuṅkāl ;
kuṉṟiṉmēṟ koṭṭun taṟipōl talitakarntu
ceṉṟiciyā vākum cevikku.
Good instruction thrown away on thankless people.
When you expound the way of virtue to ungrateful people, —which is like
mashing up sweet mangoes for a pig in a food — trough , — those virtuous
teachings lose all their force —have their point (tali) broken (takar) by
the obtuseness of the disciple— and do not enter into, or suit his ear,
— like a stake which one would drive in on the side of a hill.
258. nōlā uṭampil aṟivu nuḻiyātu.
pālāṟ kaḻīip palanāḷ uṇakkiṉum
vālitām pakkam iruntik kiruntaṉṟu;
kōlāṟ kaṭāayk kuṟiṉum pukalollā
nōlā uṭampiṟ kaṟivu.
Learning requires discipline.
Though you wash it with milk for many days and dry it, charcoal on no
hypothesis becomes white ! So into the undisciplined body wisdom enters
not, though you teach it, driving it in with a stick.
259. iḻiceyal naṭuvar nallaṟivu illār.
poḻintiṉitu nāṟiṉum pūmicital cellā
tiḻintavi kāmuṟūum īppōl — iḻintavi
tāṅkalanta neñciṉārk keṉṉākum? takkārvāyt
tēṉkalanta tēṟṟaccol tērvu.
The fly desires not the fragrant honey. The base esteem not sweet and
powerful words.
To those whose minds are full of foul things, — like the fly which goes
not to feed on the flower that pours forth sweetness and breathes
perfume, but fixes its eager desire on ordure,—What clear comprehension
can there be of the lucid words full of honied sweetness that issue from
the mouths of the worthy?
260. kīḻmakaṉ tuṇṇiya kēḷviyi virumpāṉ.
kaṟṟār urikkuṅ kacaṭaṟu nuṇkēḷvi
paṟṟātu taṉneñ cutittalāl, —maṟṟumōr
taṉpōl oruvaṉ mukanōkkit tāṉumōr
puṉkōṭṭi koḷḷumām kīḻ.
The base man rejects the words of the learned, and seeks the assembly of
congenial fools.
The base man does not apprehend the faultless words of accurate
instruction which the learned utter. These pin his mind. He therefore
looks in the face of some other one like himself (for encouragement),
and convenes a wretched assembly of his own: i.e. He finds one
like-minded, and the two set up a sect.
-------------

27. naṉṟiyil celvam / Ch. 27. Wealth that profits not

261 naṉṟiyil celvari nāṭār aṟiñar.
arukala tākip palapaḻuttak kaṇṇum
poritāḷ viḷaviṉi vāval kuṟukā;
peritaṇiya rāyiṉum vīṭilār celvam
karutum kaṭappāṭṭa taṉṟu.
Useless neighbours : so near and yet so far.
The bat approaches not the Heronia, with its dry stem, though it be
night at hand and bear abundant fruit; so although mean people are very
close at hand, their wealth is not a thing that can be counted upon.
262. celvam iruppiṉum kīḻōric cērār.
aḷḷikkoḷ vaṉṉa kuṟumukiḻa vāyiṉum
kaḷḷimēṟ kinnīṭṭār cūṭumpū aṉmiyāl
celvam perituṭiyarāyiṉum kīḻkaḷi
naḷḷār aṟivuṭi yār.
None pluck the Kalli flowers. The wise approach not the base.
Men reach not out their band to the Kalli (Cactus), though it bears
delicate round buds by the handful, becuse these are not flowers they
can weave into a garland to crown themselves withal; so wise people from
no friendships with the base however great their wealth may be.
263. īyāc celvariṭam evarum cērār.
malku tiriya kaṭaṟkōṭ ṭiruppiṉum
vallūṟ ṟuvaril kiṇaṟṟiṉkaṭ ceṉṟuṇpar :
celvam perituṭiya rāyiṉum, cēṭṭucaṉṟum
nalkuvār kaṭṭē naci.
Though living on the sea-shore, men go to the fresh spring to drink.
Though men live on the curved shore of the sea with its multitudinous
waves, they go and drink at the well, with its perennial fountin of
fresh water from the rock; so even if those (who are neighbours) are
very wealthy, the desire (of the poor) is towards the liberal, though
these may be far to seek.
264. aṟivilār celvarāy yāṇṭum vāḻkiṉṟār.
puṇarkaṭalcūḻ viyattup puṇṇiyamō vēṟē;
uṇarva tuṭiyār iruppa — uṇarvilā
vaṭṭum vaḻutuṇiyum pōlvārum vāḻvarē,
paṭṭum tukilum uṭuttu.
The senseless dressed in silks ! Virtue quite another matter.
In the world surrounded by the (all-)embracing sea, merit is quite an
indifferent matter! Understanding ones are (poor); and even those of no
understanding—who are like mere palm tree tufts and brinjals—live
prosperously, clad in silks and rich garments.
265. kallār celvattiṟkuk kāraṇam viṉiyē :
nallār nayavariruppa nayamilāk
kallārkkoṉ ṟākiya kāraṇam,— tolli
viṉippaya ṉallatu vēlneṭuṅ kaṇṇāy!
niṉippa varuvatoṉ ṟil.
Men fortunate who seem not to deserve it.
While pleasant folk and just abide (in poverty), you ponder why men
unjust and ignorant have any joy. It is fruit of 'ancient deeds', thou
whose long eyes are darts :—to thoughtful mind no other cuse occurs.
266. paṇpilār celvam payaṉillātatu.
nāṟāt takaṭēpōl naṉmalarmēṟ poṟpāvāy!
nīṟāy nilattu viḷiyarō ;—vēṟāya
puṉmakkaḷ pakkam pukuvāynī poṉpōlum
naṉmakkaḷ pakkam tuṟantu.
Fortune cursed.
O golden dame, that sittest like a scentless leaf on a beauteous flower!
Die, and fall in ashes to the ground: thou enterest homes of worthless
men of perverse mind, forsaking good men pure as gold.
267. aṟiñar vaṟumiyum aṟivilār celvamum aṭivatu viyappē.
nayavārkaṇ nalkuravu nāṇiṉṟu kollō ;
payavārkaṭ celvam parampap payiṉkol ;
viyavāykāṇ vēṟkaṇṇāy ivviraṇṭum āṅkē
nayavātu niṟkum nili.
Poverty with the good, and prosperity with the mean.
Has poverty, that bides with men of righteous souls, no shame? Does
wealth to ungenerous men stick like glue?O thou of dart-like eye, with
wonder see that thus, no just discernment made, these two abide !
268. pulliyōr celvam poruttam illātatu.
valavika ḷallātār kālāṟu ceṉṟu
kalavikaḷ uṇṭu kaḻippar — valavikaḷ
kālāṟum cellār karuṉiyāl tuyppavē
mēlāṟu pāya viruntu.
The self-dying and the self-indulgent.
Men who are not void of shame will travel forth on foot, and feed on
scraps, —so pass their days ; the shameless ones make no journeys on
foot, but feed on dinties at home, perspiring over the feast.
269. aṟivillār celvam tarupayaṉ oṉṟilli.
poṉṉiṟac cenneṟ potiyoṭu pīḷvāṭa,
miṉṉoḷir vāṉam kaṭaluḷḷum kāṉṟukukkum;
veṇmi yuṭiyār viḻuccelva meytiyakkāl
vaṇmiyu maṉṉa takittu.
Misplaced liberality; rin on the sea.
While the red paddy's golden germ is parched within the ear and dies,
the cloud gleaming with lightnings pours forth its treasures on the sea.
When silly men gin ample wealth, even so are their liberal gifts
bestowed !
270. īyār eṉṉiṟ celvarum vaṟiñarē.
ōtiyum ōtār uṇarvilār ōtātum
ōti yaṉiyār uṇarvuṭiyār : — tūytāka
nalkūrntum celvar iravā tār,celvarum
nalkūrntār īyā reṉiṉ.
The unintelligent never learn; the intelligent perceive without
learning.
Men void of understanding, though they learn, learn not! Men of
understanding, though unlearned, are as men learned! They are rich,
though utter pupers, who never beg; the rich are pupers if they bestow
nothing !
-------------

28. īyāmi /Ch. 28. Absence of Charity; or, the Miser

271. pakuttuṇṇātavar pukār mēlulakam.
naṭṭārkkum naḷḷā tavarkkum uḷavariyāl
aṭṭatu pāttuṇṭal aṭṭuṇṭal;—aṭṭa
taṭittirun tuṇṭoḻukum āvatil mākkaṭku
aṭikkumām āṇṭik katavu.
Share your food with friends and foes. To the selfish heaven's gate is
closed.
To eat your own meal, after sharing what you have cooked, to the extent
of your ability, both with those who are friends and those who are not
friends, is 'cooking and eating' (= is real house keeping). To the
good-for-nothing human beings whose habit of life it is to shut
themselves up and eat alone what they have cooked, the door of yonder
world will be shut.
272. iyaṉṟa aṟañceytāl piṟavippayaṉ eytalām.
ettuṇi yāṉum iyinta aḷaviṉāl
ciṟṟaṟañ ceytār talippaṭuvar; —maṟṟip
peruñcelvam eytiyakkāl piṉaṟitum eṉpār
aḻintār paḻikaṭalat tuḷ.
Give what you can, when you can.
Whatever the measure be, those who do even lesser acts of charity to the
measure of their power shall attin to excellence. But those who, when
they have obtined great wealth. say 'we will be wise (and give) by and
by,' are lost in a sea of guilt.
273. īyāta oruvaṉi ellārum ikaḻvar.
tuyattuk kaḻiyāṉ tuṟavōrkkoṉ ṟīkalāṉ
vittuk kaḻiyum maṭavōṉi — vitta
poruḷum avaṉi nakumē ; ulakat
taruḷum avaṉi nakum.
The miser contemptible.
The senseless man who spends not time in enjoyment (of his wealth), and
who gives nothing to pions devotees, but hoards and dies, —him his
hoarded wealth derides; him all that is gracious in the world derides.
274. īyār celvam piṉ ētilār aṭivar.
koṭuttalum tuyttalum tēṟṟā iṭukkuṭi
uḷḷattāṉ peṟṟa peruñcelvam illat
turuvuṭik kaṉṉiyarip pōlap paruvattāl
ētilāṉ tuykkap paṭum.
The miser loses what he hoards.
The great wealth obtined by the man of stritened soul, who knows not
how either to give or to enjoy, shall be enjoyed by a stranger, in due
season—like lovely virgins remining unmarried in the dwelling.
275. piṟarkyōtavar peruñcelvattiṉum cāṉṟōr vaṟumi taṉicciṟappuṭiyatu.
eṟinīrp peruṅkaṭal eyti yiruntum
aṟunīrc ciṟukiṇaṟ ṟūṟalpārt tuṇpar;
maṟumi yaṟiyātā rākkattiṉ cāṉṟōr
kaḻinal kuravē tali.
Poverty better than the wealth of those who live for this world alone.
Though they have got the mighty sea with its dashing waves (to drink
from), men wait for the stream slowly issuing from the little well,
often dry, and drink there; so the exceeding poverty of the virtuous is
preferable to the wealth of those who know not of the world to come.
276. yātavaṉ poruḷ yārkkum uriyatām.
eṉateṉa teṉṟirukkum ēḻi poruḷi
eṉateṉa teṉṟiruppaṉ yāṉum;—taṉatāyiṉ
tāṉum ataṉi vaḻaṅkāṉ payaṉtuvvāṉ ;
yāṉum ataṉi atu.
Whose is the miser's wealth?
As to the property which the wretched churl clims saying 'It is mine,
it is mine ,' I too chime in with "It is mine, it is mine;' for if it is
his, he himself spends it not, nor enjoys the benefit of it; and I, too,
neither spend nor enjoy it!
277. vaṟumiyuṭiyavar celvarpōl varuntār.
vaḻaṅkāta celvariṉ nalkūrntār uyntār ;
iḻantār eṉappaṭutal uyntār — uḻantataṉik
kāppurintār kallutalum uyntārtam kinnōvu
yāppuyntār uynta pala .
Poor men better off than churls.
The poor have escaped much from which rich men that dispense not suffer.
They have escaped the reputation of having lost (their substance). They
have escaped the toil of saving it. They have escaped (the labour of)
digging (to hide it). They have escaped the ache of hands securing it
from powerful plunderers. Many are the (sorrows) they have escaped.
278. uriya poruḷi uṭaṉpakuttu uṇṇuka.
taṉatākat tāṉkoṭāṉ; tāyat tavarum
tamatāya pōḻtē koṭāar : — taṉatāka
muṉṉē koṭuppiṉ avarkaṭiyār, tāṉkaṭiyāṉ
piṉṉi avarkoṭukkum pōḻtu.
Nothing but his own churlishness hinders the churl from being liberal.
While it was his he gave not; and his heirs, how it is theirs, give not.
Before, while it was his, if he had given, they would not have reproved
(him); and afterwards, if they had given, he would not have reproved
them! Men's own avarice is the sole reason for the lack of charity.
279.varuttiṉāṟ koṭuppar tamporuḷik kīḻmakkaṉ.
iravalar kaṉṟāka īvārāvāka
virakiṉ curappatām vaṇmi ; —virakiṉṟi
vallavar vāṉṟa vaṭiyāpōl vāyvittuk
kollac curappatām kīḻ.
Those wanting in liberal instinct give only on compulsion.
Liberality is that which yields its gifts spontaneously (from good
instinct), the askers being as the calf and the givers as the cow;
meanness yields only when put into a strit and forced, as a cow with no
good instinct gives a scanty supply (vaṭi) when strong ones press.
280. īṭṭum poruḷāl eytum pala tuyar.
īṭṭalum tuṉpamaṟ ṟīṭṭiya oṇporuḷik
kāttalum āṅkē kaṭuntuṉpam ;—kāttal
kuṟipaṭiṉ tuṉpam keṭiṉtuṉpam tuṉpak
kuṟipati maṟṟip poruḷ.
Wealth is the source of many sorrows.
Gathering it together is trouble, and even so the guarding of
resplendent wealth is severe trouble. If the guarded heap diminish, it
is trouble. If it perish, it is trouble. Wealth is trouble's very
dwelling-place!
---------------

29. iṉmi / Ch. 29. Poverty

281. poruḷillārip piṇampōlavē matippār.
attiṭṭa kūṟi ariccuṟṟi vāḻiṉum
patteṭ ṭuṭimi palaruḷḷum pāṭeytum;
otta kuṭippiṟantak kaṇṇumoṉ ṟillātār
cetta piṇattiṟ kaṭi.
Money commands respect.
Though he wraps a cloth dyed red around his loins, a dozen coins or so,
will gin (the wretch) respect among many men! The man devoid of wealth,
though born of noble race, is viler (in the world's estimation) than a
lifeless corpse !
282. irappavaṉ ellā iṭamum pukuvāṉ.
nīriṉum nuṇṇitu neyeṉpar, neyyiṉum
yārum aṟivar puki nuṭpam ; —tēriṉa,
nirappiṭumpi yāḷaṉ pukumē, pukiyum
pukaṟkariya pūḻi nuḻintu.
The insinuating mendicant.
Where water cannot enter! the more insinuating ghi glides in; and smoke
as a subtler power to penetrate than even ghi. If you look into it, the
man debased by poverty will enter hunts where smoke scarce finds a way.
283. vaṟumi yuṭiyavarpāl uṟaviṉar vārār.
kallōṅ kuyarvarimēṟ kāntaḷ malarākkāl
cellāvām cempoṟi vaṇṭiṉam ;—kollik
kalāal kiḷikaṭiyum kāṉaka nāṭa!
ilāaark killi tamar.
All abandon the needy.
When on the high hill's crags the Kanthal blooms no more, the
crimson-spotted beetle tribe seek not its boughs;—Lord of the hills
wherefrom they scare parrots with stones! — the needy have no kin.
284. celvar iṭattil ellārum cērvār.
uṇṭāya pōḻtiṉ uṭintuḻik kākampōl
toṇṭā yiravar tokupvē ; —vaṇṭāyt
tiritaruṅ kālattut tītilirō eṉpār
oruvarum ivvulakat til.
Time-serving friends.
When wealth is there, obsequious myriads will assemble, like crows
around the fallen corpse. When wealth, as the beetle wheels its flight,
is gone, no one in all the world will ask, ‘Is it well with you?'
285. āṇmi kulam kalvi aḻiyum vaṟumiyāl.
piṟanta kulammāyum; pērāṇmi māyum;
ciṟantataṅ kalviyum māyum; —kaṟaṅkaruvi
kalmēṟ karūum kaṇamali naṉṉāṭa!
iṉmi taḻuvappaṭṭārkku.
Nothing benefits the poor man.
Lord of the pleasant land of clustering hills whose crags are washed by
sounding waterfalls !—Their race is nought, their manly prowess is
nought, their rare learning is nought, when men are held in poverty's
embrace.
286. vaṟiyavaṉ vāḻviṉāṟ piṟarpayaṉ aṭiyār.
uḷkūr paciyāl uḻinaciic ceṉṟārkaṭ
kuḷḷū riruntumoṉ ṟāṟṟātāṉ, —uḷḷūr
iruntuyir koṉṉē kaḻiyātu tāṉpōy
viruntiṉaṉātalē naṉṟu.
Let the churl become a guest of others!
Although he dwells within the village, and sees the poor draw nigh with
hungry soul desiring aid, he yields them none : why then in the village
does he vinly pass his days? It were better he went to be a guest
himself !
287. vaṟumi vantāl aṟivum nīṅkum.
nīrmiyē yaṉṟi nirampa eḻuntataṅ
kūrmiyu mellām oruṅkiḻappar ;—kūrmiyiṉ
mulli alikkum eyiṟṟāy ; nirappeṉṉum
allal aṭiyappaṭṭār.
Poverty ruins all.
O thou whose teeth vie in sharpness with jasmine buds! — When sharp
distress of poverty assils, men lose all their attributes of goodness
at once, with the mind's acuteness gined from amplest stores of wisdom.
288. piṟarkku utavātavaṉ irappatē naṉṟu.
iṭṭāṟṟup paṭṭoṉ ṟirantavark kāṟṟātu
muṭṭāṟṟup paṭṭum muyaṉṟuḷḷūr vāḻtaliṉ
neṭṭāṟṟuc ceṉṟu nirimaṉiyiṟ kinnīṭṭuṅ
koṭṭāṟṟu vāḻkkiyē naṉṟu.
The struggle with want.
Better indeed is the life that pertins to the ruinous course (keṭṭa
āṟu), that going far away (neṭṭāṟu), stretches out (supplicating) hand
at every door, than to dwell at home (uḷḷūr ) toiling, subjectto
(āṟṟuppaṭṭu ) obstacles (muttu ). not giving aught to those that ask,
becuse of stritened circumstances.
289. uppilāk kīriyum uṇpār vaṟiyavar.
kaṭakam ceṟintataṅkikaḷāl vāṅki
aṭaku paṟittukkoṇ ṭaṭṭuk —kuṭikalaṉā
uppili ventitiṉṟuḷḷaṟṟu vāḻpavē,
tuppuravu ceṉṟulantak kāl.
Reverses.
The hands once loaded with golden bracelets now cull the forest-herb and
cook the meal; and then eat the mess unseasoned, from the palm-leaf for
a dish! Thus sad at heart they live, when fortune is gone and ruin come.
290. celvavāḻvu illiyēṟ cuṟṟamum illi.
ārtta poṟiya aṇikiḷar vaṇṭiṉam
pūttoḻi kompiṉmēṟ cellāvām; —nīrttaruvi
tāḻā uyarciṟappiṉ taṇkuṉṟa naṉṉāṭa!
vāḻātārk killi tamar.
Interested friends.
The humming spotted beetle tribes all bright in hue gather not on the
branch that has ceased to blow. Lord of the good cool hilly land, of
high renown, whence bounteous streams flow down unceasingly—the
unprosperous have no kin!
--------------

30. māṉam Ch. 30. Honour (Self-respect.)

291. māṉam uṭiyāṉ maṉam kaṉalpōṉṟatu.
tirumatuki yākat tiṟaṉilār ceyyum
perumitaṅ kaṇṭak kaṭittum — erimaṇṭik
kāṉan talippaṭṭa tīppōṟ kaṉalumē,
māṉa muṭiyār maṉam.
Honourable minds are wroth with wealthy arrogance.
The mind of those possessed of honourable feeling will kindle into
flame, like the fire that has cught the forest when the conflagration
rages, whenever they behold the hughty acts of those who are destitute
of virtuous habits, and to whom their wealth is their only strength.
292. uriyāṉ taṉkuṟi māṉam uṭiyavaṉ.
eṉpāy ukiṉum iyalpilār piṉ ceṉṟu
tampā ṭuripparō tammuṭiyār; —tampā(ṭu)
uriyāmi muṉṉuṇarum oṇmi yuṭiyārk(ku)
uriyārō tāmuṟṟanōy.
High-minded men complin not to the unsympathizing.
Will (honourable men who are) 'masters of themselves' follow graceless
men to tell of their sufferings, tbough fallen away to mere skeletons ?
Do they not (rather) tell the pin they have felt to those enlightened
souls that understand their sufferings before they speak ?
293. māṉam uḷḷavaṉ celvari matiyāṉ.
yāmāyiṉ emillam kāṭṭutum tāmāyiṉ
kāṇavē kaṟpaḻiyum eṉpārpōl — nāṇip
puṟaṅkaṭi vittīvar cōṟum ataṉāl
maṟantiṭuka celvar toṭarpu.
Dependence on the wealthy destroys self-respect.
As for ourselves we would introduce them (these friends) to our
household : but as regards them, they are ashamed (of us), as though
they sid, as soon as they (the ladies) saw them (the friends,) it would
be the destruction of their womanly reserve; and so they seat us at the
backdoor and give us rice! Therefore let us dismiss all thought of rich
men's friendship.
294. immiyum maṟumiyum iṉpamtarum māṉam.
immiyum naṉṟām iyalneṟiyuṅ kiviṭā
tummiyum nalla payattalāl, —cemmiyiṉ
nāṉam kamaḻum katuppiṉāy! naṉṟēkāṇ,
māṉa muṭiyār matippu.
The law of honour is that mintined by honourable men.
O thou whose locks diffuse the odour of pleasant musk! Behold,
especially good is (it to cultivate) that which is esteemed by men of
honourable mind; for in this world it is (obviously) good : and, since
it leads to perseverance in the way of virtue, it will yield good things
in yonder world also.
295. aṟiñar paḻipāvaṅkaṭku añcuvār.
pāvamum ēṉip paḻiyum paṭavaruva
cāyiṉum cāṉṟāvā ceykalār ; cātal
orunāḷ orupoḻutit tuṉpam avipōl
arukavi yāṟṟutal iṉṟu.
Dishonour worse than death.
The men 'fulfilled of excellence,' though death were the alternative, do
not deeds that entil sin and guilt. Death is an affliction for one day,
and for a little while. There is nothing that works irreparable ill like
those (deeds).
296. iravātavarē peruñ celvar eṉpavar.
mallaṉmā ñālattu vāḻpavaruḷḷellām
celva reṉiṉum koṭātavar nalkūrntār;
nalkūrntak kaṇṇum perumut tariyarē,
celvaric ceṉṟiravu tār.
The wealthy and the poor.
Poor are the men that give not, even though deemed wealthiest of all
that flourish on the teeming apple earth! They who even when they are
poor seek not as suppliants wealthy men are 'Lords of the three mighty
lands.'
297. tūyamaṉattavar coṟpaḻikku añcuvar.
kaṭiyelām kāypaci añcumaṟ ṟēṉi
iṭiyelām iṉṉāmi añcum — puṭiparanta
viṟpuruva vēlneṭuṅ kaṇṇāy! taliyellām
coṟpaḻi añci viṭum.
The honourable dread most the loss of reputation.
Thou who hast long dart-like eyes with eye-brows extending far, like a
bow! the lowest class of men dread burning hunger; the other (or middle
class) dread what is unpleasant; all the chief of men fear words that
impute crime.
298. celvar ciṟunōkkam nallōri varuttum.
nallā peritaḷiyar nalkūrntār eṉṟeḷḷic
celvar ciṟunōkku nōkkuṅkāl — kollaṉ
uliyūtun tīyēpōl uḷkaṉalum kollō
taliyāya cāṉṟōr maṉam.
The rich man's contemptuous pity.
When the very worthy and thoroughly learned see the rich men's glance of
disparagement, as they say contemptuously, 'These are good people
—persons in greatly reduced circumstances—poor folks,' does not their
mind kindle into flame within them, like the fire by the breath of the
bellows on the blacksmith's forge?
299. tamatu kulamēṉmiyum cāṟṟār māṇikaṉ.
nācciyārk kiyāmi nāṇaṉṟu; nāḷnāḷum
accattāl nāṇutal nāṇaṉṟām; —eccattil
melliya rākittam mēlāyār ceytatu
collā tiruppatu nāṇ.
What is dishonouring.
It is no shame (disgrace) not to (be able to give to those who desire it
of us. The shrinking on account of fear (felt) day by day is not shame
(modesty). But to become reduced in other ways, and not to [dare to]
tell what injuries those who love us not have inflicted on us is shame
(disgrace, or self-respect).
300. māṉaṅ keṭavariṉum vāṉulakum vēṇṭār.
kaṭamā tolicciya kāṉuṟi vēṅki
iṭam vīḻnta tuṇṇā tiṟakkum;—iṭamuṭiya
vāṉakam kiyuṟiṉum vēṇṭār viḻumiyōr,
māṉam aḻuṅka variṉ.
Heaven itself must not be sought at the expense of honour
The jungle-hunting tiger that slays the wild cow, refuses to eat and
passes by what has fallen (of itself) in its path (i. e. carrion); so
the excellent, though the wide realms of heaven were within their reach,
would not desire them, if to be obtined (only) by the loss of honour.
--------------

31. iravaccam /Ch. 31. The Dread of Mendicancy.

301. teḷinta aṟiviṉar mayaṅkiṉōr piṉcelvār.
nammālē yāvarin nalkūrntār eññāṉṟun
tammālām ākkam ilareṉṟu — tammi
maruṇṭa maṉattārpiṉ celpavō tāmum
teruṇṭa aṟivi ṉavar.
Ignorant condescending patrons.
Will men themselves possessed of clear discerning knowledge follow after
those who have yielded themselves up to mental bewilderment, and who
(erroneously) say (of suppliants), ‘These poor folks depend entirely on
us, evermore are they without resources in themselves ?'
302. iḻiceyal vāḻviṉum iṟappatu naṉṟu.
iḻittakka ceytoruvaṉ āra uṇaliṉ
paḻittakka ceyyāṉ pacittal tavaṟō
viḻittimikku māttiri yaṉṟō? oruvaṉ
aḻittup piṟakkum piṟappu.
Honest hunger and dishonest fulness of food.
Is not a man's dying and birth agin measured by the twinkling of an
eye? Is it then a fault if a man rather choose to suffer hunger, doing
no blameworthy actions, than to feed full, doing things that entil
disgrace ?
303. aṉpiṉ iṭattē mēṉmakkaḷ aṇukuvar.
illāmi kantā iravu tuṇintoruvar
cellāru mallar ciṟuneṟi ;—pullā
akampukumiṉ uṇṇumiṉ eṉpavarmāṭ ṭallāl
mukampukutal āṟṟumō mēl?
Ask only of the courteous.
There will never be wanting those who, with their destitution as their
support (making it their excuse), will venture upon mendicancy, and
tread the way of humiliation; but will the noble-minded man enter (as a
suppliant) the presence of any save of those who will embrace him and
say, 'Enter my dwelling, eat of my food ?'
304. celvarpiṉ ceṉṟu nillār nallōr.
tiruttaṉṉi nīppiṉum teyvam ceṟiṉum
urutta maṉattō ṭuyarvuḷḷi ṉallāl,
aruttam ceṟikkum aṟivilār piṉceṉ
ṟeruttiṟiñci nillātām mēl.
No reverses bend the noble spirit.
Though fortune forsake him, and fate frown, the man of lofty soul,
dwelling with steadfast mind on things above, disdins to stand with
bending neck in the trin of the foolish who hoard their wealth.
305. karavā naṇpariyum iravār mēlōr.
karavāta tiṇṇaṉpiṉ kaṇṇaṉṉār kaṇṇum.
iravātu vāḻvatām vāḻkki ;—iraviṉi
uḷḷuṅkāl uḷḷam urukumāl, eṉkolō
koḷḷuṅkāl koḷvār kuṟippu.
Mendicancy is always pinful.
To live asking nught even from those dear as an eye, whose love is
sure, and who never refuse, is happy life. Since the mind dissolves in
shame at the very thought of beggary,— when men receive alms, what are
the receivers' thoughts, I pray ?
306. ayalāri irappatāl payaṉ yātum milli.
iṉṉā iyika iṉiya oḻikeṉṟu
taṉṉiyē tāṉirappat tīrvataṟku — eṉṉikol
cātal kavaṟṟum maṉattiṉāl kaṇpāḻpaṭ
ṭēti lavari iravu.
Contentment better than mendicancy.
When a man himself, begs of himself, saying, ‘Let troubles come, let
joys depart!' the sting of poverty is extracted; why then, for this
purpose, should any one, his mind agitated with desire, and his eye
wasted with weeping, beg of strangers ?
307. irappāri ikaḻātu iruppavaṉ ciṟantōṉ.
eṉṟum putiyār piṟappiṉum ivvulakat
teṉṟum avaṉē piṟakkalāṉ — kuṉṟiṉ
parappelām poṉṉoḻukum pāyaruvi nāṭa!
irappāri eḷḷā makaṉ.
Beggars are universally despised.
Lord of the land where gold is borne down by rushing waterfalls from
every hilly slope! — Though in this world new (kinds of) men are
continually born, that very man is never born that does not scorn
mendicants.
308. īeṉa virappatu mikavum iḻivām.
puṟattuttāṉ iṉmi naliya akattuttaṉ
naṉñāṉam nīkki niṟīi oruvaṉi
īyāy eṉakkeṉ ṟirappāṉēl anniliyē
māyāṉō māṟṟi viṭiṉ.
Mendicancy is unmitigated misery.
Want wastes his outward frame; he lays aside wisdom, his inward being's
good; and so resolving, begs of some stranger, saying, 'Give to me.’— If
then the boon be refused, must he not that very instant die?
309. vaṟiñar celvaric cārntu vāḻka.
oruva roruvaric cārntoḻukal āṟṟi
vaḻipaṭutal valluta lallāl, —paricaḻintu
ceyyīrō eṉṉāṉum eṉṉuñcoṟ kiṉṉātē
piyattāṉ cellum neṟi?
A hermitage preferable to mendicancy.
Men form close intimacies with others and live as their dependants, and
this is permissible ; but is it more pinful quietly to go one's way (to
the hermitage) than, lost to all sense of personal dignity, to say,
'Will ye not do aught for me?’
310. paḻamipaṟṟik kiḻami pārāṭṭuka.
paḻamikan tākap pacinta vaḻiyē
kiḻamitāṉ yātāṉuñ ceyka ;—kiḻami
poṟāar avareṉṉiṉa pottittam neñcattu
taṟāac cuṭuvatōr tī.
Relieve the wants of old friendsthough they shrink from it.
Relying on old friendship, in an affectionate manner, render such
assistance as your intimacy Warrants. If he will not endure (the
assistance), will it not be a fire heaped up and burning in his bosom?
----------------

32. aviyaṟital / Ch. 32. The Knowledge of the Assembly

311. ciṟṟaṟiviṉar avi cērār aṟiñar.
meyññāṉak kōṭṭi uṟaḻvaḻi viṭṭāṅkōr
aññāṉan tantiṭ ṭatuvāṅ kaṟattuḻāyk
kiññāṉaṅ koṇṭoḻukuṅ kāraṟi vāḷarmuṉ
coṉñāṉam cōra viṭal.
